> ### 📌 HOW THE QUESTIONS ABOVE EACH ENTRY ARE CITED — checked 9 Nov 2026
> Of **342** question quotations in this file, **264 are the exact words of the question paper**. The other 78 are catalogued in **`10a_Citation_Corrections.md`**, with the paper's own wording beside each. Two of those 78 matter to how you write: on a 9-mark *justify* question the paper prints only an option label and the mark scheme explains what it means, so a quotation that looks like the paper's may be the scheme's. **No wrong question was found in this file.**

## WHY THIS FILE EXISTS, AND HOW IT COVERS THE WHOLE QUALIFICATION

Across 38 papers the qualification asks **governed** questions — anything needing three marks or more of written reasoning. That looks like hundreds of essays. It is not, and writing hundreds of essays would be the wrong response to it.

**The number of distinct framings is small and stable:**

| Tariff | Distinct framings | What actually varies |
|---|---|---|
| **3-mark Explain** | **8** | the **topic** |
| **6-mark Analyse** | **~6** | the **topic** and the framing |
| **9-mark Justify** | **1** — *"justify which one of these two options…"* | the **option pair** |
| **12-mark Evaluate** | **~5** | the **topic** |

**So there are only about twenty distinct framings in the whole qualification.** Every governed question is a **framing** wrapped around a **topic**.

**How many instances of each framing there actually are:**

| Tariff | Instances | Where the number comes from |
|---|---|---|
| **3-mark Explain** | **152** | ✅ **derived — 1(f) · 2(c) · 2(d) · 2(e), 4 per paper** |
| **6-mark Analyse** | **114** | ✅ **derived — 1(g) · 3(d) · 4(b), 3 per paper** |
| **9-mark Justify** | **76** | ✅ **derived — 2(f) · 3(e), 2 per paper** |
| **12-mark Evaluate** | **38** | ✅ **derived — 4(c), 1 per paper** |
| **TOTAL GOVERNED** | **380** | ✅ 152 + 114 + 76 + 38 |

> 🧮 **Coverage is now DERIVED, not carried by hand.** Run `python3 layer_c_6mark_coverage.py` from the folder above: it reads the derived inventory, checks every block against a `COVERED` map, and **also checks the other way — any entry number that does not exist in this file is printed as a dangling reference.** It was written because **Batch 5b recorded twelve blocks covered when it had covered eight**, leaving four 2019 blocks missing until the block file was re-read by hand. **That is the failure mode this bank is built to prevent, and one hand-counted total was enough.**

> ✅ **THE 6-MARK TIER IS CLOSED.** All **114 blocks** are carried by entries **101–174** — verified by `layer_c_6mark_coverage.py`, which prints **114 covered / 0 remaining** and flags any dangling entry reference. **It was 74 entries for 114 blocks**, because **40% of the tier was a repeat of a topic already answered**: *multinational* and *motivating employees* **six times each**, *break-even* **five**, and *cash*, *SoCI*, *interest rates*, *branding*, *robotics*, *induction* and *host-economy benefit* **three or four times each**.

> ✅ **THE 9-MARK TIER USES A CONSISTENT ARCHIVE FRAMING, WITH DISTINCT QUESTION PAIRS.** In the checked 2019–2026 inventory, all **76** questions use a Justify framing and matched schemes allocate AO2 = 3, AO3 = 3 and AO4 = 3. The inventory records **76 distinct ordered option pairs**; entries **175–227** group related decision topics while retaining each question's own options, case and scheme. The scaffold and groupings are navigation aids, not prescribed answer structures.

> ✅ **THE 9-MARK TIER IS CLOSED.** All **76 blocks** are carried by entries **175–227** — verified by `layer_c_9mark_coverage.py`, which prints **76 planned / 76 covered / 0 remaining**. The **53 entries** group related themes; consult each mapped question and scheme when using an entry.

> ✅ **The topic groupings cover the full checked inventory:** sources of finance · promotion · pricing · market research · success measures · job design · motivation · contracts · training · production · operations · recruitment · communication · place · product · marketing · structure.

> ✅ **THE 12-MARK TIER IS CLOSED.** All **38 blocks** are carried by entries **228–252** — verified by `layer_c_12mark_coverage.py`, which prints **38 planned / 38 covered / 0 remaining** and separates *planned* from *covered by a written entry* so that a claim made in advance can never masquerade as an answer. **It was 25 entries for 38 blocks, across 22 distinct topics** — a much smaller compression gain than the other tiers, because **the 12-mark stem usually names one topic and one business, and Pearson does not repeat them.** Three topics do carry two or three papers each (**quality → competitive advantage**, **location**, **the market**), and those entries hold a per-paper comparison table rather than three separate essays.
>
> **Entry 228 summarises the shared guidance for the checked Evaluate items.** The exact task and level descriptors govern; examples of evaluation across these schemes include trade-offs, alternative factors, conditions and challenges to an assumption. These are possible approaches, not a fixed set of routes or a required answer architecture.

> **How those four numbers were obtained, and why they can be trusted.** `build_layer_c_tariffs.py` reads the same 38 mark schemes, reconstructs every question part from its printed AO allocation, and then **checks its own work: a 4BS1 paper is worth 80 marks, and all 38 papers now sum to exactly 80.** The question labels are *invariant* — all 38 papers carry 1(f), 2(c), 2(d) and 2(e) at 3 marks, 1(g), 3(d) and 4(b) at 6 marks, 2(f) and 3(e) at 9 marks, and 4(c) at 12. Run `python3 build_layer_c_tariffs.py` to reproduce; it writes `layer_c_6_9_12_master.csv`.
>
> **These supersede the CSV's 86 / 48 / 17**, which came from question-paper text extraction. They also settle an earlier open question: the 9-mark count is not merely ≥2 on P1-family papers — **every one of the 38 papers has exactly two 9-mark Justify questions.**

> 🚨 **The old headline figure of "262 governed items" is withdrawn.** It was built on `4BS1_Question_Instances.csv`, and that file is measurably wrong. **The full scope correction is the next section — read it before trusting any count in this bank, including any count in another file.**

This file supplies both halves:

- **`01_Mark_Tariff_Playbook.md`** already holds the framings — the structure and mark allocation for each tariff.
- **This file holds the topics** — the actual causal chains that earn the marks, one entry per topic.

A topic example can help with transfer, but a prompt’s framing, case context and AO demands vary. Check each new question and its own mark scheme; one model does not certify coverage of future variants.

## HOW TO USE IT

Every entry gives you:

1. **The question as it was actually asked** — with the session and paper, so you can check it.
2. **A model answer** in the exact shape the marks are awarded for.
3. **The chain, broken out** — so you can rebuild it under pressure rather than memorise prose.
4. **A second acceptable chain**, because the mark scheme accepts *"any other appropriate response"*.

> ### A useful 3-mark Explain approach — one point, two expansions; no fixed answer shape
> For Explain, identify **one relevant fact/point** and develop it with **two relevant expansions**. The expansions may follow one another (for example, a reason leads to an effect, which leads to a further effect), or may each develop the original point in a different relevant way. Use the exact item’s MS to check the content and the task. The same explanation need not end in profit: a valid development may concern customers, staff, costs, sales, quality, risk, cash, reputation, an operational effect or another relevant consequence. There is **no fixed sentence count**, no mandatory mechanism→profit chain, no compulsory opening definition and no judgement requirement. Do not repeat the point or count the same consequence twice as separate development.
>
> ### ⭐ Keep the task and beneficiary exact
> Preserve the prompt’s requested **reason, advantage, disadvantage, method, purpose, effect** (or other task) and the named beneficiary. For example, a benefit **to a country** is not automatically a benefit **to a business**, and sponsorship benefits to the event recipient do not answer an advantage for the sponsoring business.
>
> ### ⭐ The Explain command word
> **Explain assesses AO1 — Knowledge. Context is not required.** June 2025 P1 states: *"No context/reference to the business in the exam paper is required in Explain questions."* Context may be used where it helps explain the point, but it is not a compulsory mark or answer component.

### 🚨 SCOPE CORRECTION — **152** three-mark blocks, not 111

**This file previously counted the 3-mark population from `4BS1_Question_Instances.csv` and reported 111. That number is wrong, and the fault was in the measuring instrument rather than in the counting.**

The CSV was built from **question-paper text extraction**, which is column-scrambled and silently loses whole question parts. The **mark schemes** state every part cleanly, and read that way **each of the 38 papers carries exactly four 3-mark Explain questions — 1(f), 2(c), 2(d) and 2(e):**

```
3-mark Explain blocks, read from the mark schemes : 152   (4 x 38 papers)
from 4BS1_Question_Instances.csv                  : 111   (under-count of 40)
```

How much is lost varies by paper — **May/June 2024 P1 shows 3 in the CSV but has 4; Nov 2024 P1 shows 2 and May/June 2025 P2 shows 1, and both have 4.** The shortfall is therefore **not a constant that can be added back on.**

**So `4BS1_Question_Instances.csv` is no longer the scope authority for Layer C — the mark schemes are.** The tool that re-derives this, and which also harvests each block's official worked answer, is **`../build_layer_c_3mark.py`** → **`../layer_c_3mark_master.csv`**. Both are tracked, and the tool re-reads the archive on every run, **so a sandbox re-seed cannot lose the finding again.**

### ⭐ PROGRESS — 3-mark Explain, derived and not estimated

```
3-mark blocks total      :  152
canonical topics         :   97
covered by entries 1-100 :   97 topics  ·  152 blocks
REMAINING                :    0 topics  ·    0 blocks
```

> **How this is counted.** `build_layer_c_3mark.py` reads all 38 mark schemes, harvests every 3-mark block, and normalises each to a canonical topic — *"identification of a benefit to a business of using e-newsletters"*, *"a way a business could use e-newsletters"* and *"a benefit to a business of using e-newsletters"* all resolve to **E-NEWSLETTERS**. **0 blocks are unmapped.** The figure above is **97 distinct canonical topics, all of which are now carried by an entry in this file.**
> ⚠️ **Rule 118 is now enforced by machine.** Every hand-written total in this file has drifted at least once. **This one cannot**: re-run the tool and it prints the number.

### ✅ THE 6-, 9- AND 12-MARK TOTALS ARE **DERIVED** NOW — the CSV's figures are WITHDRAWN

**This block used to say the three larger tariffs were unsafe to quote. They are not any more** — the block parser that was owed here has since been written, and it reads all 38 mark schemes:

```
6-mark Analyse : 114   (1(g) · 3(d) · 4(b) - 3 per paper)
9-mark Justify :  76   (2(f) · 3(e)   - 2 per paper)
12-mark Evaluate: 38   (4(c)         - 1 per paper)
```

**And one sentence in this block was too narrow.** It said the P1-family papers carry two Justify questions; **the derived census says every paper carries two — `2(f)` and `3(e)`** — which is why the tier is 76 blocks and not 38.

# BATCH 1 — the newest series (June 2026 and Nov 2025), all 14 three-mark topics

---

## 1. Long chain of command — disadvantage

**Asked:** *"Explain one disadvantage for a business of having a **long chain of command**."* — June 2026 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A long chain of command means instructions pass through many layers of management before reaching the people who do the work. **This slows communication down**, because each layer must relay the message on, so decisions take longer to reach the shop floor. **This means the business reacts more slowly than its competitors** to changes in customer demand, so it can lose sales it would otherwise have made.

| Step | The move |
|---|---|
| **1. Point** | A long chain = many layers between top and bottom |
| **2. Mechanism** | Each layer relays → **messages slow and can be distorted** |
| **3. Consequence** | Slower reaction to the market → **lost sales** |

**Second acceptable chain:** long chain → more managers to pay → **higher wage costs** → less profit at the same revenue.

> ⚠️ **Do not write "communication is bad".** The mark is for **why** many layers slow it down, then what that costs the business.

---

## 2. Why business aims and objectives may change over time

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why business **aims and objectives may change over time**."* — June 2026 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A business's aims may change as the market it operates in changes. **If a competitor launches a much cheaper product, a business aiming to be the market leader may switch to aiming simply to survive**, because it can no longer compete on price. **This changes where the business spends its money** — less on expansion and more on cutting costs — which keeps it trading instead of failing.

**Second acceptable chain:** the business grows → the owner's original aim of *survival* is **achieved**, so the objective moves to **growth or profit**, changing how profits are used.

> ⭐ **The chain pattern is: something changes → the old aim stops fitting → the business redirects its effort.**

---

## 3. Economies of scale — reason a business benefits

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business may **benefit from economies of scale**."* — June 2026 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Economies of scale happen when a business grows and its **average cost per unit falls**. A larger business can buy raw materials in bulk, and suppliers charge **less per unit for large orders**, so each item costs less to produce. **This raises the profit margin on every sale**, meaning the business keeps more from the same selling price — and it can undercut rivals while still profiting.

**Second acceptable chain:** growth → **specialist machinery or staff** become affordable → output per worker rises → **unit cost falls**.

> ⚠️ **Name the *source* of the saving** (bulk buying, specialisation, technology). "It gets cheaper because it's bigger" is the answer that scores 1.

---

## 4. Poor cash flow causing business failure

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why **business failure may be caused by poor cash flow**."* — June 2026 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Cash flow is the money moving in and out of a business day to day, and it is different from profit. **A business can be profitable and still fail if its money is tied up in stock and unpaid customer invoices**, so it has no cash in the bank on the day a supplier or wage bill is due. **Suppliers then refuse to deliver or the business cannot pay staff**, and trading stops even though the accounts show a profit.

**Second acceptable chain:** poor cash flow → **cannot pay debts when they fall due** → creditors take legal action or the bank withdraws the overdraft → **the business is forced to close**.

> ### ⭐ THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT DISTINCTION IN 3.5
> **Profit is not cash.** Profit is revenue minus costs over a period; cash is what is actually in the bank today. **This distinction is the whole mark.**
> ⚠️ **Note which paper this appeared on: P1.** Cash flow is not a P2-only topic.

---

## 5. Calculating break-even level of output — reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business **calculates the break-even level of output**."* — June 2026 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Break-even output is the number of units a business must sell to cover its total costs, where revenue exactly equals costs. **Calculating it tells the owner how many sales are needed before the business stops making a loss**, so they know whether their sales forecast is realistic. **This lets them set a target and decide whether to go ahead with the product** — or negotiate a lower rent or cheaper supplier before committing money.

**Second acceptable chain:** break-even output → **compare against expected demand** → if expected demand is lower than break-even, **the launch is not viable** and the money is saved.

> ⚠️ **Rule 07 note:** you do **not** have to draw or read a break-even *chart* for this — the examiner has said diagrams are not required for the calculation questions. **The formula is break-even output = fixed costs ÷ contribution**, and contribution = price − variable cost.

---

## 6. Segmenting the market by income

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business would **segment the market by income**."* — June 2026 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Dividing customers by income lets a business aim a product at the group that can actually afford it. **A business selling luxury goods targets high earners and avoids wasting money advertising to people who will never buy**, because its price point excludes them. **This makes its marketing budget go further**, so the same spend produces more sales than advertising to everyone.

**Second acceptable chain:** income segmentation → **two product versions** (premium and budget) → the business serves both ends of the market and **gains customers it would otherwise lose** to competitors.

> ### ⚠️ THE WORDS TO GET RIGHT
> *Segmentation* is **dividing the market into groups**; *targeting* is choosing which group to serve. **The question says "segment" — answer with the dividing, then the benefit of having divided.**
> ⭐ And note the examiner's warning, recorded as rule 74: **"demographic segmentation" was confused twice in 2023.** Income is one *way* of segmenting; it is not a type of research.

---

## 7. Productivity improvements — benefit to a business

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business from **productivity improvements**."* — June 2026 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Productivity is output per worker, so an improvement means **the same number of staff produce more**. The business therefore spreads its fixed costs — rent, machinery, salaried managers — across more units. **This lowers the average cost of each unit, so the business makes a bigger profit margin on every sale**, or can cut its price to win customers while still covering costs.

**Second acceptable chain:** higher productivity → **more output without more wages** → unit labour cost falls → **more competitive pricing** → higher market share.

> ⚠️ **Do not confuse productivity with production.** *Production* is how much is made; *productivity* is how much is made **per worker or per hour**. A factory can raise production by hiring, and raise productivity not at all.

---

## 8. Quality control — advantage

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage for a business of using **quality control**."* — June 2026 P2

> **Official MS worked route (June 2026 P2, 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** the business checks products at the end of the production line (1) → defective products are removed before shipment (1) → customers should be satisfied (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A business can use quality control to check products at the end of the production line. It can remove defective items before they are shipped to customers. This should help ensure that customers receive satisfactory products.

**Another possible development:** catching defects before despatch may reduce complaints or replacement costs; use one relevant route, not a required extra sentence.

> ### 🚨 THREE TRAPS, ALL DOCUMENTED
> **1.** *"Quality control will increase the competition"* — a real candidate answer, **0 marks**. Quality control does not create competitors.
> **2.** The examiner's standing complaint: *"it was **rare** to see candidates refer to quality control, **quality assurance** or other forms of TQM."* **Learn all three terms and the difference:**
> | Term | When quality is checked | Who is responsible |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Quality control** | at the **end** — inspection and removal | an inspector |
> | **Quality assurance** | **during** — built into every stage | everyone in production |
> | **TQM** | **throughout the whole business**, including suppliers | the whole organisation |
> **3.** ⚠️ **Use business terms accurately.** A June 2025 answer about TQM scored **0** because the examiner found no understanding of TQM; its reference to customer satisfaction was therefore not credited. This is an item-specific warning about the meaning used in that answer, not a requirement to begin with a definition.

---

## 9. Limited liability — advantage for a shareholder

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage for a shareholder of having limited liability."* — June 2026 P2R

**Official MS worked route (June 2026 P2R, 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** less risk to the shareholder (1) → they can lose only the amount invested (1) → they will not lose personal assets (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A shareholder faces less risk because they can lose only the amount invested in the business. If the business fails, they will not lose personal assets such as their home or savings to repay its debts.

**Also asked about a different beneficiary:** *"Explain one advantage to a business of having limited liability."* — November 2024 P1, 1(f). Its MS includes separate business-oriented routes: separate legal entity can protect owners' personal assets if the business incurs debt; or selling shares may raise finance for expansion because shareholders' risk is limited.

> ⚠️ Both cited schemes say no marks are awarded for a definition on their respective items. This is item-specific guidance; it does not make an opening definition universally forbidden. Keep shareholder benefits distinct from business benefits: raising finance is a business outcome, not the requested direct benefit to a shareholder. The Nov 2024 report's beneficiary/task is not the June 2026 P2R prompt.

---

## 10. Temporary employees — advantage for a business

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage for a business of using temporary employees."* — June 2026 P2R

**Official MS worked routes (June 2026 P2R, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** flexibility (1) → when demand is low, the business can reduce the number of workers (1) → lower costs (1); or temporary employees may bring specialist/innovative skills (1) → help resolve specific problems/challenges (1) → maintain productivity and efficiency (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Temporary employees give a business flexibility. When demand is low, the business can reduce its temporary workforce. This can lower labour costs while less staff are needed.

> Use the question's requested **business advantage**. Do not assume a particular busy season, redundancy arrangement or wage saving unless supported by the paper/MS.

---

## 11. On-the-job training — advantage for a business

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage of on-the-job training for a business."* — June 2026 P2R

**Official MS worked routes (June 2026 P2R, 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** relatively cheap training (1) → no travel costs/training is provided by another employee (1) → business can reduce training costs/save money (1); or employees gain practical skills/knowledge efficiently (1) → learn in the work environment (1) → may improve workplace productivity (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> On-the-job training may be relatively cheap for a business. It can be delivered by another employee, so the business may not need to pay for staff travel to an external course. This can reduce training costs.

> ### ⚠️ Keep the beneficiary exact
> The prompt asks for an advantage **for a business**. A trainee-side point should be linked to a business benefit if used. The separate report note about candidates describing training rather than explaining it is item-specific; do not turn it into a fixed sentence or mechanism rule.

---

## 12. High market share — advantage

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage to a business of having **high market share**."* — Nov 2025 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Market share is the proportion of total sales in a market that one business holds. **A business with high market share is the one customers think of first**, so it gets more repeat custom without having to advertise for every sale. **This means its selling costs per unit are lower than its rivals'**, and it can often negotiate better terms from suppliers because it buys in the largest volumes.

**Second acceptable chain:** high market share → **dominant shelf space and distribution** → competitors find it harder to reach customers → **the business can protect its prices rather than discount**.

> ⚠️ **Don't say "it means more customers".** That restates market share. The mark is for **what high share lets the business *do*** — spend less to get each sale, and buy on better terms.

---

## 13. E-commerce — advantage of selling through it

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage to a business of using **e-commerce to sell its products**."* — Nov 2025 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> E-commerce means selling through a website or app rather than only from a physical shop. **The business is not limited to customers who can travel to its premises**, so it can sell to anyone with an internet connection, including overseas buyers. **This widens the market without the cost of opening more shops**, so sales can grow while rent and staffing stay roughly the same.

**Second acceptable chain:** e-commerce → **open 24 hours with no staff on the tills at night** → sales happen when the shop would be shut → **more revenue from the same stock**.

> ⚠️ **The mark is not "more customers".** It is the **shop-less reach** — cheaper distribution than physical expansion. That is the causal step.

---

## 14. Mass marketing — advantage

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage to a business of **mass marketing**."* — Nov 2025 P2

> **Official MS worked route (Nov 2025 P2, 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** more sales may be generated (1) because the product is promoted to a large number of people (1), which may improve the business’s profitability (1).

> **Constructed model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED; NOT AN AWARDED CANDIDATE SCRIPT]`
> Mass marketing promotes a product to a large number of people. This broad reach may generate more sales for the business. The additional sales may improve its profitability, although the result depends on customer response and the costs of promotion.

**Other possible development:** broader promotion may raise awareness; do not assume that every person in a mass market is interested or that lower unit costs automatically follow.

---

## 15. Social objective — advantage for a business

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage to a business of having a **social objective**."* — Nov 2025 P2

> **Official MS worked routes (Nov 2025 P2, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** a social objective may give the business a good reputation (1) through a positive impact on society (1), resulting in more potential customers (1); or it may attract employees (1) interested in environmental problems (1), widening the talent/skills available to the business (1).

> **Constructed model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A business with a social objective may create a positive impact on society. This could improve its reputation and attract more potential customers. Alternatively, an objective such as reducing environmental harm may attract employees with relevant interests or skills, widening the talent pool. These outcomes depend on how the business pursues its objective.

> ### ⚠️ Keep the task exact
> The question asks for an **advantage to the business**. Do not replace the business’s benefit with a description of the social aim or an effect only on society. The MS-supported routes are reputation/customer interest or attracting employees; no opening definition or willingness-to-pay claim is required.

---

## 16. Break-even chart — limitation

**Asked:** *"Explain one **limitation** of a **break-even chart**."* — Nov 2025 P2

> **Official MS worked route (Nov 2025 P2, 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** break-even is based on predictions (1), which may result in an incorrect forecast (1), leading to wasted resources (1).

> **Constructed model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A break-even chart is based on predicted costs, prices and output. If actual sales or costs differ from those predictions, the chart may give an inaccurate forecast of the output needed to break even. A business relying on that forecast could commit resources to a plan that is not viable.

> ### ⚠️ Keep the task exact
> This prompt asks for a **limitation of the chart**, not a general disadvantage of operating a business. An inaccurate forecast and the resulting risk of wasted resources follow the MS route; do not treat the chart as a guaranteed prediction.

---

# WHAT BATCH 1 ESTABLISHES

The answers above illustrate recurring reasoning moves: identify a relevant point, explain how it matters, and connect it to a plausible business effect. This is a flexible way to check whether an explanation is developed, not a required three-sentence form. The consequence depends on the question and may concern costs, sales, cash flow, quality, staff, customers, reputation, risk or another relevant outcome; the list is illustrative, not exhaustive.

---

| **1** | Newest 3-mark topics — June 2026 (11) + Nov 2025 (5) | ✅ **This file** |
| **2** | Historical 3-mark topics, by spec section 3.1–3.2 | ⬜ next |
| **3** | Historical 3-mark topics, 3.3–3.5 | ⬜ |
| **4** | 6-mark Analyse chains | ⬜ |
| **5–6** | 9-mark Justify option pairs | ⬜ |
| **7** | 12-mark Evaluate topics | ⬜ |

**Remaining: 246 of 262 governed items.**

# BATCH 2 — Spec 3.1 (Business activity) and the 3.2 structure cluster

**33 entries.** Related prompts (including partnership and HR-function questions) are grouped by recurring business reasoning to support transfer. Their wording, context, mark tariff and AO demand can differ; this thematic grouping does not mean the exact same response fits every instance. Check each question’s source and mark scheme.

---

# 3.1 — BUSINESS ACTIVITY AND THE EXTERNAL ENVIRONMENT

## 17. Partnership — one **advantage**

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage of **a partnership**"* — May 2023 P1R · *"…of being **in a partnership**"* — Nov 2021 P1 · *"…of being in a partnership"* — June 2024 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A partnership is owned by **two or more people** who share the capital, the work and the profits. Because each partner puts money in, **the business has more capital than a sole trader could raise alone**, so it can afford better equipment or a second premises much sooner. **This lets it grow faster than a one-owner business**, and each partner also risks less than if they had funded it alone.

**Second chain:** partners bring **different skills** → one may handle finance while another handles customers → **decisions are better and fewer mistakes are made**.

> ### 🚨 THE ERROR TO AVOID
> **A partnership does NOT have limited liability.** Partners are personally responsible for the business's debts — this is an **unlimited liability** business form. **Writing "partners have limited liability" is factually wrong and will not score.** The advantages are **more capital · shared workload · combined skills**. Nothing else.

---

## 18. Partnership — one **disadvantage**

**Asked:** *"Explain one disadvantage of being **in a partnership**"* — June 2020 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> In a partnership the owners have **unlimited liability**, which means they are personally responsible for the business's debts. **If the business fails, each partner may have to sell personal assets — even their own home — to pay what is owed.** **This makes a partnership far riskier than a limited company**, so some people will not join one and others take on less risk than they otherwise would.

**Second chain:** profits are **shared between the partners** → each partner keeps less than a sole trader would → **less personal reward** for the same effort.

**Third chain:** decisions must be **agreed between partners** → disagreements slow decisions down → **the business reacts slowly to customers**.

> ⚠️ **2019 P1R recorded this exactly:** *"One third of candidates gained a mark for identifying a disadvantage of being in a partnership but then they would **not develop their answers**."* **Identify → explain why it matters → say what it costs.**

---

## 19. Sole trader — one **advantage**

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage of being a **sole trader**."* — June 2022 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A sole trader is **one person who owns and runs the whole business**. Because there are no other owners, **the owner keeps all of the profit** rather than sharing it. **This makes them more motivated to work hard and to keep costs low**, which raises the amount the business earns, and it also means they can make decisions instantly without asking anyone.

**Second chain:** the sole trader **makes every decision alone** → changes can be made immediately → **the business responds to customers faster than a larger firm**.

> ⚠️ **The mirror trap.** If the question asks about **disadvantages** of a sole trader, the answers run the other way: unlimited liability, harder to raise finance, the workload rests on one person, no one to cover illness. **Learn both sides of every business form** — the examiner flips the question word deliberately.

---

## 20. Franchisee — one **advantage**

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage of being a **franchisee**."* — Summer 2019 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A franchisee pays the franchisor for the right to trade under **an established brand** and sell its proven products. **Customers already recognise the name and know what to expect**, so they buy from it from the day it opens, instead of the business having to build a reputation from nothing. **This means sales build far faster than for an independent start-up**, and the franchisor provides training and national advertising that a new owner could not afford alone.

**Second chain:** the franchisor supplies **proven products, equipment and staff training** → the franchisee avoids the mistakes of a brand-new business → **a much lower risk of failing than starting up independently**.

> ### 🚨 THE EXACT TRAP THE EXAMINER RECORDED
> Summer 2019 P1R: *"Some candidates **explained what a franchisee was but did not provide any advantage** of being a franchisee therefore **not gaining any marks**."*
> **A definition is not a benefit.** Open with the advantage, use the definition only as the mechanism. Only **one third** of candidates gained full marks here.

---

## 21. Multinational — one **benefit**

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit of being a **multinational business**"* — Nov 2021 P2 · *"Explain one advantage of **becoming** a multinational business"* — May 2023 P2R. These are related but distinct prompts; the P2R item is specifically about an advantage of becoming multinational.

**Official MS routes (May 2023 P2R 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** global supplier base (1) → range of components/products (1) → possible higher revenue (1); or operating across countries (1) → easier movement of products (1) → reduced trade barriers (1). The MS says no marks for a definition on this item. **Awarded candidate evidence:** the local May 2023 P2R examiner report prints a 3/3 response on expanding the market, developing that to more customers, more sales and revenue; this is a Pearson-awarded response, not an MS route.

> **Constructed model — May 2023 P2R 1(f)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A multinational business may have access to suppliers in different countries. This can give it a wider range of components for its products. That wider supply base may help the business increase its revenue.

A second MS-aligned route is that a multinational operates in multiple countries, which can make products easier to move between them as trade barriers are reduced. These routes are examples, not required phrasing. The Nov 2021 P2 report's cohort comment about insufficient development is commentary on that separate prompt; it does not impose a fixed answer length or business outcome here.

---

## 22. Globalisation — one **benefit to a small business**

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit of **globalisation to a small business**."* — Nov 2023 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Globalisation means **countries trade more freely with each other** and customers can buy from abroad more easily. **A small business can now sell through its website to customers in other countries** without opening premises overseas, so its market is not limited to the town it sits in. **This means its sales can grow far beyond what local demand would allow**, while its costs stay those of a small operation.

**Second chain:** globalisation gives a small business access to **cheaper overseas suppliers** → its material costs fall → **it can compete on price with larger rivals**.

> ### ⚠️ RELEVANCE BY PARTY — read the noun
> The question says **a small business**. A **large** business is a different answer (Nov 2023's examiner noted candidates who wrote about **large** firms' concerns). Recorded as **rule 102**, the fault appears **five times** in the reports. **Circle the party before you plan.**

---

## 23. Trading bloc — one **advantage for a country**

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage to a country of being a member of a trading bloc."* — May–June 2024 P2R 2(c); related but different item: Nov 2024 P2 2(e), *"Explain one advantage for a country of being in a trading bloc."*

**Official MS routes (May–June 2024 P2R 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** membership gives access to other markets in the bloc (1) → prices between countries may be competitive (1) because no additional costs are involved (1); or consumers have wider choice (1) because goods move freely with limited trade restrictions (1) between countries (1). The November 2024 P2 MS is separate: trade without tariff barriers → no additional costs → prices could be lower, and also accepts wider consumer choice from free movement.

> **Constructed model — May–June 2024 P2R 2(c)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Membership gives a country access to the other markets in its trading bloc. With no additional trade costs between member countries, prices may be more competitive. Consumers in the country may benefit from the lower prices.

This model follows one route; the alternative MS route is wider consumer choice. Both questions ask for an advantage to a country, not only to a business. Keep the distinct sessions and MS examples identified.

**Also asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business of being located within a trading bloc."* — Nov 2025 P2, 2(e). **Different beneficiary and application:** the MS allows a business to increase sales because the bloc encourages trade between member countries, widening its pool of customers; it also allows lower import-material costs if tariffs are reduced or removed.

**Official MS worked routes (Nov 2025 P2, 2(e); AO1, 3 marks):** potential to increase sales (1) → bloc encourages trade between member countries (1) → wider pool of customers (1); or imported materials may cost less (1) → tariffs likely reduced/eliminated (1) → business may increase profit (1).

> **Constructed model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED; NOT AN AWARDED CANDIDATE SCRIPT]`
> A business located within a trading bloc may be able to sell to customers in other member countries. Because the bloc encourages trade between its members, the business has a wider potential customer base than in its home market alone. It may therefore increase sales. Alternatively, reduced tariffs on imported materials may lower its costs and potentially increase profit.

> ### 🚨 RELEVANCE BY PARTY AND THE 2024 ERROR
> Keep the two tasks distinct: the 2024 question asks for an advantage **to a country**; the 2025 question asks for a benefit **to a business located within a bloc**. Do not swap their beneficiaries or carry one answer over unchanged.
> ⚠️ Elsewhere in the retained Nov 2024 P2 commentary, the examiner reports confusion of the term “trading bloc” with protectionism rather than liberalised trade. That is a paper-level concept warning, not a verdict specifically on this three-mark item. A bloc reduces/removes barriers between members; do not describe barriers being raised.

---

## 24. Business failure — one **reason**

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why businesses fail."* — May–June 2021 P2 2(c) · *"Explain one reason why some businesses fail."* — May–June 2025 P2R 2(c). Both are AO1 Explain (3), but their matched MS examples differ.

**Official MS route (May–June 2021 P2):** cash-flow problems → outflow exceeds inflow → potential difficulty paying employees/suppliers.

**Official MS routes (May–June 2025 P2R):** “Poor cashflow can lead to failure (1) from a lack of finance (1) to pay suppliers and other debtors (1)”; alternatively, prices too high/not competitive (1) → demand falls (1) → lack of revenue (1). The quoted “other debtors” is preserved verbatim and is an **apparent MS terminology anomaly**: in standard accounting usage, debtors generally owe the business, while the business owes suppliers/creditors. **Teaching clarification (not Pearson wording):** for the concept, use “suppliers and other creditors/payables.” This clarification does not amend the source quotation or MS awards. MS says no marks for a definition on these items.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2025 P2R 2(c)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> If a business’s prices are too high to compete with other businesses, customers may buy fewer of its products. This can reduce demand. The business may then lack the revenue it needs to continue operating.

**Pearson-awarded 2025 response:** the P2R ER p.15 awards 3 marks to a candidate identifying failure to innovate, then developing that old products are no longer what people want and that products/profits decline. Script, comment and score were visually checked; see `External_Exemplars/2025-06_P2_P2R_Explain_3mark.md`. This is an accepted alternative candidate route, not an MS bullet or a required profit endpoint. Keep the P1R item mentioned elsewhere in legacy material outside this P2/P2R review.

---

## 25. Not achieving predicted growth — one **disadvantage**

**Asked:** *"Explain one disadvantage for a business that **does not achieve its predicted growth**."* — June 2021 P2

**Official MS routes (May–June 2021 P2, 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** shareholders may be unhappy (1) → they may not receive dividends if predicted growth is missed (1) → they may invest in other businesses (1); or the business may not be able to expand (1) → it may lose market share (1) → customers could go elsewhere (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> If a business does not achieve its predicted growth, it may be unable to expand. It may then lose market share. Some customers could go elsewhere.

> These are the official routes for this disadvantage prompt. Other consequences should not replace them as if they were the MS example, and an answer need not end with profit.

---

## 26. Interest rates — an **increase**

**Asked:** *"Explain one impact on a business of an increase in interest rates."* — May–June 2024 P2R 2(e); the same broad wording appears in June 2020 P1, a separate out-of-scope item.

**Official MS routes (May–June 2024 P2R 2(e); AO1, 3 marks):** financing costs rise (1) → profits may fall (1) → growth/expansion may be affected (1); or customers have less money to spend (1) → they may buy only essentials (1) → business revenue may fall (1).

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2024 P2R 2(e)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> An increase in interest rates can raise a business’s financing costs. Higher costs may reduce the business’s profit. With lower profit, the business may have less ability to grow or expand.

The customer-spending route is an alternative official example. These routes answer this business-impact task; the June 2020 P1 wording and answer are not used as evidence for the reviewed P2R item. No additional profit endpoint is compulsory beyond a relevant developed impact.

---

## 27. Interest rates — banks **reduce** them

**Asked:** *"Explain one advantage for **businesses** if **banks reduce their interest rates**."* — June 2022 P2R

**Official MS routes (May–June 2022 P2R, 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** if a business takes out loans (1) → monthly repayments would be lower when rates fall (1) → leaving more funds for other business expenses (1); or customers may have more funds available (1) → to spend on goods/products (1) → increasing business turnover (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> If a business has taken out a loan, lower interest rates may reduce its monthly repayments. It will then have more funds available for other business expenses.

> The 2022 prompt asks for an advantage for businesses when banks reduce rates. The MS also permits a customer-spending route linked to business turnover; no fixed profit endpoint is required.
>
> **Pearson ER evidence (May–June 2022 P2R, p.12; transcribed script/verdict visually checked against the local report):** one candidate response was awarded 3 marks for paying less to the bank, saving money and using it to improve the business. The ER says many candidates confused advantage and disadvantage and warns against answering about customers instead of businesses. Keep that cohort comment distinct from the MS, which separately accepts a route through customers' additional spending and increased business turnover.

**Related but distinct item (May–June 2025 P2, 2(e)):** *“Explain one effect on a business if interest rates are decreased.”* Its own MS routes are cheaper business borrowing → cheaper finance for new projects/expansion → improved profitability; or increased consumer spending → consumers buy additional items sold by the business → increased sales income.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2025 P2 2(e)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A decrease in interest rates can reduce the cost of borrowing for a business. It may then be cheaper to finance a new project or expansion. This could improve the business’s profitability.

**Pearson ER evidence (May–June 2025 P2, p.17):** a candidate says a business may be more likely to take out bank loans but does not develop the point to explain consequences for the business; the report says 2025 candidates discussed lower borrowing costs or higher customer disposable income. The page was visually checked. This is item-specific feedback, not a requirement to describe both routes or end all Explain answers with profit.

---

## 28. Tariffs — one **effect on a business importing goods**

**Asked:** *"Explain one way that the **introduction of tariffs** might affect a business **importing goods**."* — June 2025 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A tariff is **a tax charged on goods coming into the country**. A business that imports its raw materials must now pay that tax on every delivery, so **the landed cost of each unit rises even though nothing about the product has changed**. **This forces the business either to raise its prices — and risk losing customers to cheaper domestic rivals — or to accept a lower profit margin on every sale.**

**Second chain:** imported components become dearer → the business **looks for domestic suppliers instead** → it may lose the quality or specification it relied on, or pay more while it changes supply.

> ### 🚨 A REAL 0-MARK ANSWER, QUOTED IN THE REPORT
> *"With tariffs, importing goods may be **cheaper**, allowing this business to import more at a lower cost… Therefore, potentially increasing revenue."*
> **Examiner: "This answer incorrectly addresses the question. 0 marks."**
> **A tariff RAISES the cost of imports.** Getting this direction backwards scores nothing, however well written the rest is. **Check the direction of every economic effect before you build on it.**

---

## 29. Complying with health and safety legislation — one **reason**

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business **complies with health and safety legislation**."* — June 2022 P2

**Official MS routes (June 2022 P2, 2(e); AO1, 3 marks):** non-compliance may lead to a fine or lawsuit after an accident/injury (1) → the business could be fined or closed for not complying (1); or protect staff from injuries (1) → fewer days lost to illness (1) → reduced need for temporary cover and related costs (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A business may comply with health and safety legislation to protect its staff from injuries. This may reduce the number of days lost to illness. The business may then need less temporary cover, saving costs.

> This is a **reason** prompt. Avoid turning “because it is the law” into a compulsory opening or assuming one route must end in reputation, compensation, or profit; use a relevant development.

---

# 3.2 — PEOPLE IN BUSINESS: structure and the HR function

## 30. The human resource function — purpose and responsibilities

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why **many businesses have a human resource functional area**"* — Summer 2019 P1 · *"…one **responsibility** of the human resources function"* — June 2020 P1R · *"…one reason why many businesses have a **human resource function**"* — June 2024 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> The human resource function is **the department responsible for the people a business employs** — recruiting them, writing their contracts, training them and handling discipline. **Because one department looks after every employee in the same way, recruitment and pay are handled consistently and fairly**, so the business is less likely to break employment law and face a tribunal. **This means managers in other departments can concentrate on production and sales instead of staffing problems.**

**Second chain:** HR **forecasts how many staff with which skills the business will need** → it can recruit and train ahead of demand → **the business is not short of the right people when it expands**.

> ### 🚨 THE KNOWLEDGE GAP THE EXAMINER NAMED TWICE
> Summer 2019 P1 **Q02b** was a **State** question on a function of the finance area — *"**less than one fifth** of candidates were able to correctly identify"* it.
> November 2023 P1 Q2(b) — a task of an HR employee — ***"only one tenth of candidates"*** identified one.
> **These are 1-mark questions being missed by 80–90% of candidates.** ⭐ **Know concrete tasks for each function**, not vague labels:
> | Function | Concrete tasks to have ready |
> |---|---|
> | **Human resources** | recruiting · writing job descriptions and contracts · arranging training · handling discipline and grievances · staff welfare |
> | **Finance** | keeping accounts · preparing the cash flow forecast · paying wages · arranging loans · calculating costs and profit |
> | **Marketing** | market research · designing the marketing mix · advertising campaigns · branding and packaging |
> | **Operations** | production scheduling · stock control · quality · machinery maintenance |

---

## 31. Short **span** of control — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business would have a **short span of control**."* — Summer 2019 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A span of control is **the number of employees one manager directly supervises**. With a short span the manager oversees only a few, so **each worker gets close attention, and problems are spotted and corrected before they become costly**. **This suits complex, skilled or dangerous work**, where a mistake is expensive or unsafe, and it also means staff can be trained and supported individually — which raises quality and reduces accidents.

**Second chain:** a short span means **more managers are needed** to cover the same staff → **each employee is answerable to someone clearly** → instructions and responsibility are unambiguous.

> ### 🚨 SPAN OF CONTROL IS NOT CHAIN OF COMMAND
> | Term | What it counts |
> |---|---|
> | **Span of control** | how many staff **one manager** supervises directly |
> | **Chain of command** | how many **layers** of management sit between the top and the bottom |
> ⚠️ **The examiner recorded the confusion:** November 2023 P1 — ***"some candidates confused this [span of control] with hierarchical levels within an organisation structure."***
> ⭐ **And check the direction.** This entry is *why a business would **choose** a short span* — a deliberate design decision for close supervision. The mirror (a business has a short span because it is **small**) is a different answer and less good.

---

## 32. Short **chain** of command — one benefit

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business of having a short chain of command."* — May–June 2019 P2 2(e) and May–June 2024 P2 2(d).

**Official MS route (May–June 2024 P2 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** communication is faster (1) because there are fewer levels to go through (1), so fewer mistakes may be made (1); or workers and managers work closely together (1) → managers are less remote (1) → queries can be answered quickly (1). The 2019 P2 MS route differs in wording: less communication between workers/managers → fewer mistaken messages → managers can resolve problems more quickly.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2024 P2 2(d)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A short chain of command can make communication quicker because messages pass through fewer levels. This may reduce the chance that messages are misunderstood. As a result, fewer mistakes may be made.

**Awarded-script evidence:** the local June 2024 P2 ER p.22 shows a 3/3 candidate explaining faster communication through fewer hierarchy layers and quicker messages, with fewer mistakes as the consequence. Script, examiner comment and score were visually checked; see `External_Exemplars/2024-06_P2_TUI.md`. The model and script are examples, not a mandatory fixed chain or endpoint.

---

## 33. Flat organisational structure — one benefit

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business of having a **flat organisational structure**."* — June 2022 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A flat structure has **very few levels of management and wide spans of control**, so most staff report to one or two managers. **Because there are fewer managers to pay, the business's wage bill is lower** and more of its revenue is left as profit. **It also means employees are given more responsibility for their own work**, which raises motivation and lets decisions be made quickly by the people closest to the customer.

**Second chain:** staff in a flat structure **can speak directly to senior managers** → good ideas reach decision-makers → **the business improves faster than one where ideas must climb a hierarchy**.

> ⚠️ **Do not confuse "flat" with "small".** A large business can deliberately choose a flat structure to cut overheads — **the question asks about the structure, not the size**.

---

## 34. Decentralised organisation — one advantage

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business that has a **decentralised organisational structure**"* — May 2023 P1 · *"…one advantage to a business of having a **decentralised organisation**"* — June 2024 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Decentralisation means **authority to make decisions is passed down to managers in each region, branch or department** rather than kept at head office. **Local managers know their own customers, staff and competitors**, so the decisions they take fit the local market far better than one rule applied everywhere. **This improves customer service and speeds up decisions**, because a local manager does not have to wait for approval from head office.

**Second chain:** responsibility is pushed down → **local managers feel trusted and are more motivated** → staff stay longer and perform better.

> ⭐ **The mirror is centralisation** — consistency across branches, one clear brand, easier control, bulk buying. **Know both.** ⚠️ The examiner's recurring complaint about this topic: candidates describe **what** decentralisation is and never reach **what it gives the business**.

---

## 35. Improving communication with employees — one benefit

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business of **improving communication with its employees**."* — June 2022 P1R

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> When a business communicates well, **every employee knows what is expected of them and why it matters**. **This reduces misunderstanding and mistakes** — work is done right the first time instead of being repeated, saving materials and time. **It also makes staff feel informed and valued, so they are more motivated**, and motivated staff work more productively and are less likely to leave.

**Second chain:** better communication lets **staff raise problems early** → faults are fixed before they reach the customer → **fewer complaints and returns**.

> ⚠️ **This is a benefit question, so every sentence must be a *gain*.** If you write *"poor communication causes mistakes"*, you are answering the **impact of ineffective communication** — the next entry — not this one.

---

## 36. Ineffective communication — one **impact**

**Asked:** *"Explain one impact of **ineffective communication** on a business."* — May 2023 P1R · *"…one impact on a business of ineffective communication"* — June 2024 P1

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Ineffective communication means **a message is misunderstood, delayed or never delivered**. **Employees then carry out work that does not match what was asked for** — the wrong quantity, the wrong specification or the wrong deadline — and the work has to be done again. **This wastes materials, labour and time, raising the cost of every unit**, and if the error reaches the customer it causes complaints and lost future sales.

**Second chain:** messages are delayed → **decisions cannot be made when they are needed** → the business misses opportunities such as a supplier's deadline or a seasonal selling window.

> ⭐ **The verified June 2025 answer that scored 3/3 used exactly this shape:** *"Messages being delayed or sent to the wrong receiver might cause decision making to slow down. This can cause the business to **miss out on opportunities such as securing a deal with a new supplier**."* **Generic → specific consequence.**

---

## 37. Using a **letter** to communicate with an employee — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why an employer might use a letter to communicate with an employee."* — May–June 2025 P2R 1(f).

**Official MS routes (AO1, 3 marks):** letters are a private way to communicate (1) a job offer/promotion/suspension/redundancy (1) to confirm the next stage (1); or letters are formal communication (1) → portray a professional business image (1) → are personal to an employee (1). The MS says no marks are awarded for a definition on this item.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2025 P2R 1(f)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> An employer may use a letter to communicate a promotion to an employee. A letter is a private way to communicate this change. It can confirm the next stage of the process to the employee.

**Pearson ER evidence:** on printed p.8 the report reproduces a candidate using a letter for sensitive/personal information and saying it may not be appropriate to share by text or other communication. The examiner says this identifies and develops one reason but has no further development for the third mark. The script/comment were visually checked; see `External_Exemplars/2025-06_P2_P2R_Explain_3mark.md`. This is item-specific evidence; a letter need not be framed as a tribunal record or as the only appropriate channel.

---

## 38. Health and safety training **as part of induction** — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why employees should undertake **health and safety training as part of their induction programme**."* — June 2020 P2

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> An induction programme is **the training a new employee receives in their first days**, before they work unsupervised. **If safety training happens then, the employee never has a chance to learn unsafe habits**, because they are taught the correct procedure from the very first time they use the equipment. **This reduces accidents to new staff — who are the most likely to have them — cutting lost working days, compensation claims and higher insurance premiums.**

**Second chain:** induction safety training is a **recorded, legal duty** → the business can prove it trained the employee → **it is protected from prosecution if an accident does occur**.

> ⭐ **The reason to put it in *induction* specifically is that new starters are the highest-risk group.** A generic answer about safety training misses the word the examiner put in the question.

---

## 39. Equal opportunities — one way businesses ensure them

**Asked:** *"Explain one way businesses ensure that **all employees have equal opportunities**."* — May 2023 P2R

**Official MS routes (May 2023 P2R 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** employ both men and women (1) → make training open to everyone (1) → avoid disadvantaging anyone (1); or pay employees the same salary (1) when they do the same/similar work (1) → avoid discrimination (1). This prompt asks for one way all employees have equal opportunities; the separate June 2026 P2 question asks why a business must follow legal controls.

> **Constructed model — May 2023 P2R 2(d)** `[CONSTRUCTED; NOT A PEARSON WORKED ANSWER]`
> A business could use the same job description and selection criteria for every applicant. That helps ensure decisions are based on relevant skills rather than personal characteristics. It may also help the business recruit from a wider pool of suitable applicants.

The ER's 2-mark script on p.20 uses team meetings to identify and fix issues; the examiner says the additional promotion point is a second way, while the task asks for one. The transcription and examiner comment were visually checked against the local primary page. This example does not make team meetings the only acceptable method.

> ⚠️ **"One way" asks for a method, not a reason for legal compliance.** Keep the requested task and beneficiary clear.

**Also asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business needs to follow legal controls for equal opportunities."* — June 2026 P2, 2(e). This is a **reason**, not a method of ensuring equal opportunities.

**Official MS worked route (June 2026 P2, 2(e); AO1, 3 marks):** the business may be fined by government/courts (1) → if it does not treat employees fairly/equally (1) → resulting in poor business reputation (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A business may be fined by the government or courts for not treating employees fairly or equally. This legal penalty could harm the business’s reputation. A poor reputation may make it harder to attract employees or customers.

The fine and reputation route is the official example; the final effect is a constructed elaboration. A definition of equal opportunities is not required for this question.

**Also asked:** *"Explain one reason why businesses comply with equal opportunities legislation."* — May–June 2024 P2R 1(f). This is a separate legal-compliance reason prompt, not the May 2023 method prompt.

**Official MS routes (2024 P2R 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** compliance with the legal requirement → cannot discriminate on grounds such as gender/race/age → business could be taken to court/sued; or non-compliance with legislation → employers may be liable to be sued. The MS says no definition marks on this item.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2024 P2R 1(f)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Businesses must comply with equal opportunities law. This means they cannot discriminate against employees on grounds such as gender, race or age. A business that does not comply could be taken to court and sued.

---

## 40. Supporting employees with disabilities — one way

**Related prompts, kept distinct:** *"Explain one way businesses support employees with disabilities."* — May–June 2024 P2R 2(d); *"Explain one reason why businesses employ people with disabilities."* — May–June 2024 P2 2(c). The P2R item asks for a support method; the P2 item asks for a reason to employ.

**Official MS routes — May–June 2024 P2R 2(d), support method (AO1, 3 marks):** avoid discrimination against disabled employees (1) → employ a person with limited mobility (1) → make reasonable adjustments to premises (1); or a disability should not affect promotion prospects (1) → an employee applies for internal promotion (1) → receive the same treatment as other candidates (1).

> **Constructed model — May–June 2024 P2R 2(d)** `[CONSTRUCTED; NOT A PEARSON WORKED ANSWER]`
> A business can make reasonable adjustments to its premises for an employee with limited mobility. For example, it could adapt access to the workplace. This can help the employee carry out their work without being disadvantaged by the premises.

**Official MS routes — May–June 2024 P2 2(c), reason to employ (AO1, 3 marks):** employing people with disabilities gives them an equal opportunity to work (1) → improves their lifestyle (1) → develops their skills (1); or the business must follow government legislation (1) → if not, it may fail to comply with the law (1) → it can be sued (1). This is a different task from supporting current employees.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2024 P2 2(c)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Employing people with disabilities gives them an equal opportunity to work. This may improve their lifestyle. It can also help them develop their skills.

**Pearson-awarded P2 evidence:** the June 2024 P2 ER p.20 gives 3/3 to a candidate who says employing someone with a disability can improve the business’s public image, attract customers willing to support it, and increase sales. The script/comment/score were visually checked against the local ER. This is a candidate’s accepted answer, distinct from both MS routes and the constructed P2R model.

> ⚠️ Preserve the prompt's task and party: “way to support” is not interchangeable with “reason to employ.” Neither model nor candidate script creates a compulsory legal or commercial endpoint.

---

## 41. Full-time employees — one benefit

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit for a business of having **full-time employees**."* — June 2020 P2

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> A full-time employee works **all the business's normal hours every week**, rather than for a fixed short period. **Because they are always there, they build up detailed knowledge of the products, the customers and the procedures**, so they handle unusual situations without being supervised and make fewer mistakes. **This gives customers consistency and the business reliable cover**, and the employer repays the commitment with loyalty — so fewer staff leave and recruitment and training costs fall.

**Second chain:** full-time staff can be **given responsibility and promotion** → the business develops future supervisors from within → **it saves the cost of recruiting experienced managers externally**.

> ⚠️ **Contrast with entry 42 (seasonal staff) and Batch 1 entry 10 (temporary employees).** All three are employment-type questions and the examiner flips between them. **The variable is the trade-off: permanence for reliability, versus flexibility for cost.**

---

## 42. Seasonal staff — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason for a business **using seasonal staff**."* — June 2020 P2

> **Model answer — 3 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> Seasonal staff are taken on **only for the part of the year when demand peaks**, such as a shop at Christmas or a hotel in summer. **The business can serve far more customers in its busiest weeks** without turning them away, so it captures sales it would otherwise lose. **And when the season ends the contracts finish**, so the business is not paying wages through the quiet months or making redundancies it cannot afford.

**Second chain:** seasonal workers are often **experienced in that season's work** → they can be useful with very little training → **the business avoids the cost of a long induction**.

> ⭐ **Two halves, and the second is the one candidates forget: gain the peak sales, then lose the cost in the trough.** A one-sided answer about "more staff to help" is worth 1 mark.

---

## 43. Retaining employees — one benefit

**Asked:** *"Explain one benefit to a business of **retaining its employees**."* — Summer 2019 P2R

**Official MS route (May/June 2019 P2R, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** retaining staff provides consistency (1) → employees gain knowledge of the products they sell (1) → they can pass this information to customers (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Retaining employees gives a business consistency. Employees who stay can build up knowledge of the products they sell. They can pass that knowledge on to customers.

> A cost-saving or customer-service route may also be relevant when supported; neither is a compulsory endpoint for this prompt.

---

## 44. Fully trained employees — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business has **fully trained employees**."* — June 2022 P2R

**Official MS routes (May–June 2022 P2R, 2(d); AO1, 3 marks):** training increases employees' skills and knowledge (1) → they perform their job more effectively (1) → productivity increases (1); or training keeps employees up to date with new technology/machines (1) → they can use these effectively (1) → maximise output per hour (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Training can increase employees' skills and knowledge. They may then perform their jobs more effectively. This can increase productivity.

> The task asks for a reason the business has fully trained employees. Use one relevant training benefit and two developments; the exact MS routes above are examples, not a required sentence pattern.
>
> **Pearson ER evidence (May–June 2022 P2R, pp.21–22; transcribed scripts/verdicts visually checked against the local report):** two separate candidate responses were awarded 3 marks. The examiner comments on avoiding mistakes/maintaining quality/customer satisfaction in one, and reduced safety risks, fewer mistakes and employee safety in the other. These are awarded script examples, not additional MS wording or a fixed answer template.

---

## 45. Employees keeping their **CV up to date** — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why **employees keep their curriculum vitae (CV) up to date**."* — June 2021 P2

**Official MS route (May–June 2021 P2, 2(e); AO1, 3 marks):** an employee can apply for a job straight away (1) → without spending time updating a CV (1) → avoiding missed job opportunities (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Keeping a CV up to date means an employee can apply for a job straight away. They do not have to spend time updating it first. This may help them avoid missing out on job opportunities.

> The prompt asks why **employees** keep their CVs current. Keep the explanation on the employee’s benefit; no particular job-loss scenario is required.

---

## 46. Motivating employees — one benefit

**In-scope prompt:** *"Explain one method a business could use to motivate its employees."* — May–June 2022 P2R, 2(c). **Related but out-of-scope prompt:** *"Explain one benefit for a business of motivating its employees."* — June 2022 P1R; not reviewed here.

**Official MS routes (May–June 2022 P2R, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** give employees fringe benefits (1) → for example discounts on the business's products (1) → employees feel their work is valued (1); or make bonus payments (1) → to individuals or teams (1) → when they meet specified targets (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> One method is to give employees fringe benefits, such as discounts on the business's products. This can make employees feel that their work is valued.

> The question asks for a **method** of motivation, not a benefit of motivation. The separate June 2022 P1R prompt asks about a business benefit and is outside this P2/P2R review; it is not evidence for the P2R item's task or answer. No single method or sentence pattern is compulsory.
>
> **Pearson ER evidence (May–June 2022 P2R, pp.19–20; the transcribed scripts/verdicts were visually checked against the local report):** the report awards 2 marks to a job-rotation response, noting the method and change in workplace but insufficient further development; a separate job-enrichment response earns 3 marks, with comments on challenge and employees feeling trusted/valued. This candidate evidence is distinct from the MS's fringe-benefit/bonus routes and does not create a required “business outcome” endpoint for all Explain answers.

---

## 47. Herzberg's motivational theory — one factor

**Asked:** *"Explain one of the factors in Herzberg’s motivational theory."* — May–June 2024 P2 1(f); related June 2020 P2 item has the same broad prompt.

**Official MS routes (May–June 2024 P2 1(f); AO1, 3 marks):** a hygiene factor, such as pay (1) → gives employees security that they can pay bills (1) → further relevant explanation (1); or a motivator factor (1) → employees gain achievement at work (1) → this allows growth and development (1). The MS accepts any appropriate response and says no marks are awarded for a definition on this item.

> **MS-aligned model — May–June 2024 P2 1(f)** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Achievement is a motivator in Herzberg’s theory. An employee who achieves something at work may feel a greater sense of accomplishment. This can help them grow and develop in their role.

The examiner report treats the two concepts as hygiene and motivational factors and says to explain one factor, not list them. A 2-mark hygiene-factor candidate response appears on p.11; a full-mark response with the report comment appears on p.12. The selected pages were visually checked; their page-specific evidence is transcribed in `External_Exemplars/2024-06_P2_TUI.md`. No business-profit endpoint, fixed sequence or sentence count is required by this item.

---

## 48. Fringe benefits — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business uses **fringe benefits**."* — Summer 2019 P2

**Official MS route (May/June 2019 P2, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** employees will be motivated (1) → this could improve productivity (1) → and increase sales (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> A business may use fringe benefits to motivate its employees. Motivated employees may be more productive. This could help the business achieve higher sales.

> The question asks why **a business** uses fringe benefits; connect the point to the stated business purpose. An effect on an employee alone does not complete the explanation.

> **Separate, out-of-scope report excerpt:** the retained June 2026 P1R example below is not evidence for this May/June 2019 P2 item and was not used to assess it.
> *"One impact of using fringe benefits to their employees is that it reduces their salaries which can demotivate the employees resulting in a loss for business…"* The retained transcription attributes 0 marks because it says the response attempted an employee-side impact. The quotation/verdict is not independently re-verified here.

---

## 49. Paying above the minimum wage — one reason

**Asked:** *"Explain one reason why a business **would pay its employees above the minimum wage**."* — June 2022 P2

**Official MS routes (May–June 2022 P2, 2(c); AO1, 3 marks):** paying above the minimum wage may encourage more employees to work for the business (1) → they could be paid more than at a competitor (1) → they may stay because of the payment (1); or employees feel more rewarded (1) → this may increase motivation (1) → and productivity (1).

> **MS-aligned model answer — 3 marks** `[MS-ALIGNED MODEL ANSWER]`
> Employees may feel more rewarded when a business pays above the minimum wage. This could increase their motivation at work. Higher motivation may improve their productivity.

> This prompt asks for a reason to pay above the minimum wage. No context, definition, named motivational theory, or particular final business outcome is required by the question.

---

# BATCH 3 — Spec 3.3 (Marketing)

**23 entries.** Marketing is the largest remaining cluster: 33 of the 152 three-mark blocks sit in 3.3, in 23 distinct topics.

> ## ⭐⭐ A NEW LABEL, AND WHY IT IS NEEDED
> Batches 1 and 2 were `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` throughout — **and as of 14 Oct 2026 every one of those entries carries the label on the entry itself, not only in this sentence. 49 of them (entries 1–49) had been covered by the batch heading alone; `verify_chain_bank_anchors.py` now fails if any model answer in this file is unlabelled, because a file that mixes all three kinds has to be readable one entry at a time.** **Batch 3 is different: for almost every entry below, Pearson's own mark scheme prints a worked answer** — the *"Award 1 mark for … (1) … (1) … (1)"* model that the scheme accepts as a full-mark response.
> That is a **third source category**, and the labelling contract has only two labels, so this batch uses a third and defines it:
>
> | Label | What it is | What it is NOT |
> |---|---|---|
> | `[VERIFIED CANDIDATE EXEMPLAR]` | a real student script that an official Pearson report scores at full marks (session, paper, question, page cited) | — |
> | **`[OFFICIAL MARK-SCHEME WORKED ANSWER]`** | **Pearson's own indicative content, quoted verbatim, carrying the scheme's own (1)(1)(1) allocation** | **not a candidate script · not examiner-marked · no candidate wrote it** |
> | `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` | engineered by me from QP + MS + ER | not official, not a student script, not examiner-marked |
>
> **Every entry below states which of its chains come from the scheme and which are mine.** Where a chain is labelled *scheme*, the (1) markers are Pearson's, and the route is one the scheme explicitly accepts. **This still does not make it a marked script** — it is the scheme's own model, which is the strongest thing that exists for a 3-mark question, but it is a different kind of evidence and it is labelled as such.

> ### Scope note: definitions in particular Explain mark schemes
> Some of the cited MSs explicitly state “No marks are awarded for a definition” for the question at hand. In those items, a bare definition does not answer the task; explain the requested method, reason or benefit.
