I would choose competition pricing only if the cost and rival-price comparison supports it; if Huawei needs to overcome brand-switching reluctance and can sustain the discount, promotional pricing may be more effective. The QP supplies no cost, rival-price or demand figures.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective/context** | Launch a new smartphone in a very competitive market; the QP gives no numeric objective beyond the launch decision. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Comparable price may attract consideration; cheaper promotion may induce brand switching and market-share gains. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Competition pricing may not cover cost; promotional sales may decline after the offer ends. |
| **Evaluation** | Decide from cost coverage and likely persistence of switching; neither method guarantees profit or repeat sales. |

**Alternative chain:** use promotional pricing if a lower launch price is needed to overcome customers’ commitment to current brands and Huawei can afford the reduced price; assess whether buyers remain after normal pricing resumes.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** keep the objective and two pricing methods tied to Huawei’s smartphone launch. Do not transfer Tata’s cost-plus argument or treat “realistic” as proof that costs are covered.

---

## 198. PRICING — an introductory price vs relying on brand recognition

**Asked:** *"Option 1: offer an introductory price to entice shoppers to try the popcorn · Option 2: rely on customers recognising the farm's name when comparing to its competition."* — **May–June 2021 P1, 2(f)**, NOF

> **This is the only pair in the tier where one option is not a price at all.** **Option 2 is the decision to charge a normal price and let the brand do the work** — so the question is really: **is the brand strong enough to skip the discount?**

**The AO2 anchors:** an introductory price **could entice shoppers in the farm shop to try the popcorn**; customers **might recognise the farm's name so will buy the popcorn when comparing to its competition**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **NOF should use the introductory price.** The decisive fact is **what the popcorn is: a product the farm has never sold before, sitting on a shelf beside brands that shoppers already know.** **NOF's name is recognised for the FARM — for its honey and its rooms — and "as they do not have an established brand of popcorn, customers may choose to buy a more popular branded popcorn they know".** **Recognition of the business does not transfer automatically to a new product category**, which is the assumption option 2 makes. **An introductory price gives the shopper a reason to take the risk on the unfamiliar bag**, and **trying it could help convert the farm's reputation into popcorn sales.** **However, "the profit on each bag of popcorn will be low even though sales may increase, so in the short term profits might suffer"** — and that is a real cost, paid before any benefit arrives. **I would still choose the introductory price, on the condition that it is time-limited to the season the popcorn launches in — because a permanent low price on popcorn would simply become the price of popcorn, while a temporary one ends once the farm's reputation has done the work.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The introductory price** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | The farm's **name is not a popcorn brand** → recognition **does not transfer to a new category** → a reason to try is needed |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Introductory price → **low profit per bag, short-term profits suffer**; brand route → **customers may choose a known brand instead** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided it is time-limited to the launch season** |

**Second acceptable chain:** an introductory price **brings shoppers into the farm shop where the honey and rooms are also sold** → **the loss on the popcorn is recovered on the rest of the basket** → which is why a farm shop can afford a loss-leader a standalone popcorn brand could not.

> ⭐ **The idea to take away: brand recognition is SEGMENT-SPECIFIC.** *"Customers know the farm"* is not *"customers know the popcorn"*. **Wherever a question asks you to rely on an existing reputation to sell something new, that gap is your argument.**

---

## 199. PRICING — cost plus vs price skimming

**QP wording (LEGO):** *"LEGO is constantly introducing new toys to the market to keep its customers buying. It is planning to introduce a new Star Wars Princess Leia toy. It is considering the following two pricing strategies to launch the toy. Option 1: cost plus. Option 2: skimming. (f) Justify which one of these two options LEGO should choose."* — **May–June 2022 P2, 2(f)**.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording; MS PDF p.13):** cost plus is easy to apply and covers the manufacturing cost when the toy sells; LEGO earns profit on each sale. Skimming sets a high release price; the MS says the high margin may still yield high profits if only a few sell because Star Wars toys are popular. The counterpoints are that customers may wait for cost-plus prices to fall, while skimming’s high price may limit potential customers.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose cost-plus pricing for the Princess Leia toy if LEGO’s priority is to keep a broad group of customers buying its new toys.** The MS says LEGO will know that “the cost of manufacturing the Princess Leia toy is covered when sold,” and that it can identify a profit on each sale. Price skimming means the toy is sold at a high price when released; the MS notes the high margin may still produce high profits if fewer units sell because Star Wars toys are popular. However, the high price may limit the number of potential customers, which could work against LEGO’s stated aim of keeping customers buying. Cost plus also has a risk: customers may wait until its price falls, delaying sales. I would favour cost plus if its price is one intended buyers will accept while covering costs; if demand at launch is strong enough for customers to accept a high release price, skimming could instead raise margin. The QP provides no price, cost or demand figures to settle that condition.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Launch a new toy as part of LEGO’s stated aim of keeping customers buying. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Cost plus covers manufacturing cost on each sale; skimming sets a high release price and may yield high margin on fewer sales, with popularity as an MS illustration. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Customers may wait for the cost-plus price to fall; skimming may exclude potential customers because the price is too high. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Compare likely demand at the release price with the aim of maintaining purchases; no numerical evidence is supplied. |

**Alternative chain:** if LEGO’s evidence shows strong demand among customers willing to pay the high launch price, skimming may yield higher margin while the Star Wars toy is popular. If the high price is likely to deter many buyers, that same feature conflicts with the QP’s “keep its customers buying” objective.

**Saha (November 2024 P1) is a separate case and remains pending its own review; do not transfer this LEGO model or conclusion to it.**

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** connect the price choice to LEGO’s stated customer-purchase objective. The MS’s popularity point supports a possible skimming case, but neither fan willingness to pay nor a launch-price window is quantified in the QP.

---

## 200. PRICING — competition pricing vs cost plus

**Asked:** *"Option 1: competition pricing at similar prices to other clothing retailers · Option 2: cost plus with a markup for profit."* — **Nov 2023 P1, 3(e)**, AWG ($£$*Icelandic jumpers made from sustainable materials*)

> **This is the pricing question the bank most wants you to see, because the correct answer turns on a single fact buried in the case: the manufacturing cost is HIGH.**

**The AO2 anchors:** competition pricing would see AWG **selling clothing at similar prices to other clothing retailers**; cost plus would **calculate the costs of making the jumpers and add a markup for profit**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **AWG should use cost plus pricing.** The decisive fact is **what AWG makes its jumpers from: sustainable materials, which are more expensive than the conventional ones its competitors use.** **Competition pricing means matching other clothing retailers — but those retailers are not paying AWG's material costs, so a price that suits them may be below AWG's own cost of production.** **The scheme states it plainly: "as AWG use sustainable materials in its jumpers, the manufacturing cost of the clothing may be high, which means selling at a competitive price may not be feasible."** **Cost plus starts from AWG's real costs and adds a markup, so the business cannot accidentally sell below what it spent** — which is the minimum any business needs before it can think about profit. **However, cost plus removes the market from the decision: it "does not account for what the customer is willing to pay, and AWG could be losing out on possible increased revenue and profits."** **I would still choose cost plus, on the condition that AWG makes the sustainability itself the reason to pay the higher price — because the materials cost more, and if customers do not value the difference, AWG is paying a premium for a feature nobody is buying.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **Cost plus** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **Sustainable materials cost more than rivals'** → matching the competition **may price below cost** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Cost plus → **ignores what the customer will pay, losing potential revenue**; competition pricing → **may not be feasible at AWG's cost base** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the sustainability is made the reason to pay more** |

**Second acceptable chain:** competition pricing **removes price as a reason for a shopper to choose a rival** → AWG competes on the product instead → **but only if the rival's price is above AWG's cost, which is exactly what the sustainable materials prevent.**

> ⭐ **This entry is the proof of the bank's central claim about this tier: the OPTION PAIR does not determine the answer — the CASE does.** **Cost plus is the "safe but unimaginative" option here; on Nando's or Huawei it would be the wrong choice.** **Read the case fact that makes the difference, then decide.**

---

## 201. PRICING — price matching vs a discount

**QP wording:** *"To be competitive in India, Premier Inn must make sure that it uses the correct pricing strategy. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: competition pricing. Option 2: promotional pricing. (f) Justify which one of these two options Premier Inn should choose."* — **November 2024 P2, 2(f)** (printed p.10; identifier P75961RA01020).

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording; MS p.15):** competition pricing matches competitors’ room prices and may attract customers at a similar/slightly lower price; promotional pricing may discount a three-night stay and increase bookings/revenue. Risks: customers may prefer another hotel’s facilities; more bookings do not guarantee equal or higher profit. AO2/AO3/AO4 = 3 each; Level 3 requires detailed application, developed analysis and a clear judgement based on thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose competition pricing for Premier Inn in India if a comparable room price makes its hotels attractive against local alternatives.** Matching competitors may encourage customers to book when the price is similar or slightly lower. A discount on a three-night stay may also increase bookings and revenue, but it gives up some revenue per booking and does not guarantee that profit stays the same or rises. Competition pricing has its own limitation: a customer may still choose another hotel for its facilities, so matching price alone may not win the booking. I would choose competition pricing if the matched price covers costs and Premier Inn’s facilities are competitive; if it needs to attract customers for a longer stay and the discounted bookings remain profitable, the three-night promotion may be better. The QP provides no competitor prices, facility comparisons or cost data.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective/context** | Use the correct pricing strategy to compete in India. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Match rival room prices to attract bookings; a three-night discount may raise bookings and revenue. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Customers may prefer other facilities; increased bookings do not ensure equal/higher profit. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare room costs, local rival prices and facilities; none is quantified in the QP. |

**Alternative chain:** a three-night promotion may be more effective if the discount brings additional bookings that would otherwise go elsewhere and the resulting contribution still covers costs; the MS warns that higher bookings do not guarantee higher profit.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** distinguish competition pricing from a time-limited discount. Keep both the facility-choice risk and profit uncertainty in view; do not assume room-comparison behaviour or midweek demand not given by the case.

---

## 202. PROMOTION — a free giveaway vs mass advertising

**QP wording:** *"Apple wants to increase its sales in India. Apple is considering the following two options: Option 1: increase its spending on TV advertisements. Option 2: offer university students free AirPods (wireless headphones) when purchasing a MacBook laptop. (f) Justify which one of these two options Apple should choose to increase sales in India."* — **June 2026 P2R, 2(f)**.

**MS illustrations (not QP wording):** the MS says a TV advert may target a large audience and create awareness of Apple products/brand throughout India; the AirPods offer may encourage some young people or students to buy an Apple laptop rather than a competitor. Its counterpoints are that many people, especially young people, may not listen to or be interested in TV ads, and that students may not value the promotional product or may prefer cheaper competitors such as Huawei.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose the free AirPods offer for university students buying a MacBook.** The question is about increasing Apple’s sales in India. The MS suggests the offer may persuade some students to choose an Apple laptop rather than a competitor, so it can link the promotion directly to a purchase. TV advertising could also raise awareness of Apple products across India and encourage sales, but the MS warns that many people—especially young people—may not listen to or be interested in TV advertisements, so the campaign may not reach its intended audience cost-effectively. The AirPods offer is not certain to work: some students may not value the headphones, may consider the offer insufficient, or may prefer cheaper products such as Huawei. I would favour the offer if university students are a significant target segment and value AirPods; if Apple needs broader awareness among customers across India, TV advertising may be more suitable. The QP and MS provide no audience-size figures, so the relative reach or sales effect cannot be quantified.

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Choice / objective** | AirPods with a MacBook for university students, as a possible route to higher sales in India. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | TV could raise awareness across India; the AirPods offer may persuade some students to choose an Apple laptop over a competitor. Neither effect is guaranteed. |
| **AO4 comparison** | TV may miss some of its intended young audience and be cost-ineffective; students may not value the AirPods offer or may prefer a cheaper competitor. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Compare the intended segment and likely customer preferences. The source gives no audience size or sales data to prove which promotion reaches more likely buyers. |

**Alternative chain:** if Apple’s immediate sales objective depends on awareness among customers beyond university students, TV advertising could be preferable because the MS says it can target a large audience and create awareness across India. That is a conditional option, not evidence that a particular audience size watches the advertisements.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** connect the choice to sales in India and the relevant buyers. Keep the MS’s “large audience” and “some young people” as illustrative possibilities; do not turn them into claims that all young buyers ignore TV or that TV’s effective audience is a known size.

---

## 203. PROMOTION — paid celebrity endorsement vs a free giveaway

**QP wording:** *"Samsung competes for market share with businesses such as Apple and Xiaomi in the technology market. Samsung plans to increase its promotional spending to increase its market share. Samsung is considering the following two options: Option 1: Sponsor celebrities to use its products. Option 2: Provide a free game app to customers when they purchase a product. (f) Justify which one of these two options Samsung should choose."* — **November 2025 P2, 2(f)** (printed p.11; identifier P78971A01120).

The stated objective is to increase Samsung’s market share by increasing promotional spending. A celebrity endorsement and a free game app are distinct promotional routes; do not import a universal “gift beats celebrity” rule.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording; MS p.14):** Samsung could pay a celebrity to use its smartphones for publicity; people may be influenced by celebrities. Free game apps may encourage young people to choose Samsung over Apple and may be an attractive offer. Risks: a chosen celebrity’s scandal could damage Samsung’s reputation and demand; some customers may care more about other phone features/apps than free games. AO2/AO3/AO4 = 3 each; Level 3 requires detailed application, logical analysis and a thoroughly evaluated judgement.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose the free game app if Samsung’s target customers value that offer when choosing a phone.** The MS says the apps may encourage young people to choose Samsung rather than Apple and that some consumers may see them as an attractive special offer. Celebrity sponsorship may also increase publicity: people who see an admired celebrity using a Samsung smartphone may be encouraged to do the same. However, Samsung could suffer reputational damage and lower demand if its chosen celebrity is involved in a scandal. The free-game offer has a different risk: some customers may care more about other phone features or apps. I would favour the game app if it is relevant to the customers Samsung wants to attract; if celebrity influence is stronger in the target market and Samsung can manage the association risk, sponsorship may gain more attention. The QP gives no audience or response data.

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Increase Samsung’s market share through promotional spending. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Celebrity use may create publicity and influence buyers; free apps may attract young customers away from Apple. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Celebrity scandal may damage reputation/demand; some buyers may value other phone features/apps more than games. |
| **Evaluation** | Base the choice on target-customer response and the manageability of the endorsement risk; no audience data is supplied. |

**Alternative chain:** celebrity sponsorship may be preferable if the relevant customers are strongly influenced by the chosen celebrity and the association is credible; evaluate the possible scandal risk against that reach.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** compare market-share reach and buyer persuasion with the two actual MS risks. Celebrity endorsement is not automatically short-lived or unsafe, and a free app is not automatically more valuable to every buyer.

---

## 204. PROMOTION — sponsoring a charity or event vs special offers

**QP wording:** *"Kraft Heinz constantly aims to promote its products to encourage people to increase sales. To do this Kraft Heinz is considering using the following techniques: Option 1: sponsorship of a local charity. Option 2: increasing the number of special offers. (f) Justify which one of these two options Kraft Heinz should choose."* — **November 2023 P2, 2(f)** (printed p.11; identifier P74635A01124).
**Also asked:** *"Option 1: free trial of a new dog food brand · Option 2: sponsor a local dog show."* — **June 2024 P1, 2(f)**, PPS

**MS illustrations (Kraft Heinz; MS p.15, not additional QP wording):** sponsorship of a local charity such as Animal Rescue may generate publicity and local-community involvement; notices may lead customers to support Kraft Heinz. More special offers on sauces may encourage purchases and help sales/competitiveness. Risks: charity sponsorship does not guarantee product purchases; offers may increase costs that are not recovered. AO2/AO3/AO4 = 3 each; Level 3 requires detailed application, logical analysis and a clear, thoroughly evaluated judgement.
**The AO2 anchors (PPS):** a free trial **will appeal to dog owners who regularly purchase dog food**; sponsoring a dog show **will promote products such as dog collars**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose more special offers if Kraft Heinz needs to encourage purchases of its sauces directly.** The MS says offers may increase purchases and help keep the business ahead of competitors. Sponsoring a local charity such as Animal Rescue may create publicity and community involvement, and people seeing event notices may want to support Kraft Heinz. But that publicity does not guarantee that they buy its products. Special offers are more directly linked to the stated sales aim, although they may cost more than the additional sales recover. I would choose offers when the expected extra contribution justifies their cost; if local-community awareness is the larger problem and the sponsorship cost is affordable, the charity option may support goodwill and future interest. The QP gives no offer cost, redemption or sponsorship-reach data, so neither sales effect is certain.

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Promote products to increase sales. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Charity sponsorship may build local publicity/support; more sauce offers may encourage purchases and sales. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Sponsorship may not lead to purchases; special offers may have unrecovered costs. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare the size/cost of incremental sales with sponsorship reach and affordability; no figures are supplied. |

**Alternative chain (Kraft Heinz):** charity sponsorship may be preferable where local awareness and community association are the main barriers to sales, provided that the publicity reaches relevant customers and the cost is proportionate. **Second acceptable chain (PPS):** sponsoring a **dog show** puts the products in front of **owners who already buy dog food and collars** — a targeted audience at a moment they are thinking about their dog → **but "it does cost to sponsor an event so PPS would need to incur cost upfront before they gain an increase in revenue", which a small business may not be able to carry.**

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** more special offers are directly connected to the QP’s sales objective, but their unrecovered-cost risk matters; local sponsorship may build publicity without guaranteeing purchases. The MS’s examples are possible routes, not prescribed conclusions.

---

## 205. MARKET RESEARCH — primary research versus secondary research

**Asked five times in six years:**
- *"Option 1: questionnaires to existing and potential customers face to face · Option 2: the internet, to gain wider sources of information about the area it wishes to expand into."* — **May–June 2019 P1R, 3(e)**, Lavender
- **Tata Motor Company, May–June 2019 P2, 3(e), QP wording:** *"Tata Motor Company gathers data to help it promote its vehicles. It is considering two options: Option 1: primary research. Option 2: secondary research. Justify which one of these two options Tata Motor Company should use."* The direct-information and car-manufacturer-websites wording below comes from that item’s MS, not the QP stem.
- *"Option 1: questionnaires, approaching potential customers face to face to see what will attract them to visit the farm · Option 2: view a large selection of websites to look at other farms."* — **May–June 2021 P1, 3(e)**, NOF
- *"Option 1: the internet, to view a wide variety of customers' comments about their experiences · Option 2: customers complete a form at their leisure, giving details of the experiences they received."* — **June 2024 P2, 3(e)**, TUI
- *"Option 1: hand out questionnaires to customers that visit the shop · Option 2: using the internet to research new flower trends."* — **May 2023 P1R, 3(e)**, Aude Rose

> **Note how the same decision is written five different ways, and that the option ORDER changes.** TUI's question puts the internet first. **Read the options, not the pattern.**

**The AO2 anchors (Lavender):** primary research **allows Lavender to approach existing and potential customers face to face**; secondary research **allows it to gain wider sources of information about the area it wishes to expand into**.
**The AO2 anchors (Aude Rose):** questionnaires give **feedback straight away from customers buying flowers in the shop**; the internet lets it **view a large selection of flower-based websites**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on Aude Rose; the structure transfers, the case facts do not)*
> **Aude Rose should use the questionnaires.** The decisive fact is **what the business is trying to find out and from whom: whether customers will buy a new range, which only its own customers can answer.** **Its customers are standing in the shop, buying flowers** — the scheme's own point is that this gives **"feedback straight away from customers when buying flowers"**, so the research and the customers are in the same place at the same time, at no travel cost. **Internet research may provide broad information about flowers and trends, but it may be less specific to Aude Rose's own customers than asking them directly.** **The scheme's objection is decisive here: "whilst this research can give information about up-and-coming trends, it gives no specific information on the demand for the new trends by its existing customers, so could be a waste of time."** **Trends that exist in general may not be wanted by the people who actually walk into the shop.** **However, the questionnaire has its own weakness and it is a serious one: "customers may not know about the new trends, so cannot give any constructive feedback on the purpose of the research being conducted."** **Asking someone about something they have never considered produces an opinion, not evidence.** **I would still choose the questionnaires, on the condition that they are shown the new range rather than asked to imagine it — because the method only works if the customer has something real to react to.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The questionnaires** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | The customers are **in the shop, buying flowers** → **immediate, relevant feedback** on **whether they will buy the new range** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Internet → **gives trends but nothing about this shop's customers → may waste time**; questionnaires → **customers may know nothing about the trends** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided customers are shown the range rather than asked to imagine it** |

### Tata Motor Company — item-specific constructed answer (May–June 2019 P2, 3(e))

**QP wording:** *"Tata Motor Company gathers data to help it promote its vehicles. It is considering two options: Option 1: primary research. Option 2: secondary research. (e) Justify which one of these two options Tata Motor Company should use."* QP p.12.

**MS illustrations (PDF p.15, not QP wording):** primary research can obtain information directly from people about cars they want and the price they would pay; secondary research is easier to collate from car-manufacturer websites and reports. The MS links primary research to relevant information for designing/pricing future cars, and secondary research to faster collection. Its AO4 cautions are primary research’s time/manpower cost and secondary data possibly not answering Tata’s needs.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose primary research if Tata needs evidence from potential customers to shape how it promotes its vehicles.** Asking people directly about the cars they want and the prices they are prepared to pay could give Tata information relevant to designing and pricing future models, which may help it present vehicles that buyers are more likely to consider. Secondary research from car-manufacturer websites and reports may be quicker to gather, but the information may not answer Tata’s particular questions about its intended customers. Primary research has a real cost in time and manpower, so Tata should keep the questions focused and target people who could buy the vehicle. I would prefer primary research when customer preferences and willingness to pay are central to the promotion decision; if Tata needs broad background information quickly, secondary research may be the more practical first step. The QP gives no budget, time limit or target-customer profile, so it does not establish that one method is always superior.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Gather data to help promote Tata vehicles. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Direct research can reveal desired cars and prices; that information may guide design/pricing. Secondary sources can be collated faster but may be less specific. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Primary research costs time and manpower; secondary data may not answer Tata’s actual question. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Match the research method to the decision, sample and time/budget available; none are supplied in the QP. |

**The same decision, five different case-specific considerations — useful examples, not required answer ingredients:**

| Business | What the research is FOR | So a relevant consideration may be |
|---|---|---|
| **Lavender** | expanding into **other parts of Malaysia** | *provided the questions are pitched at customers in the area being considered, not the ones already served* |
| **Tata** | designing and **pricing the next cars** | *provided the sample covers the buyers of the segment the new car is aimed at* |
| **NOF** | **what would attract visitors to the farm** | *provided it is asked of people who have never visited — existing customers already know* |
| **TUI** | **improving a day trip** | *provided the form is handed out on the trip itself, while the experience is fresh* |
| **Aude Rose** | a **new range** | *provided the customers are shown the range* |

> ⭐ **Five blocks, one decision, five different evaluative considerations.** These examples show how case-linked evaluation can vary; they do not establish a required condition formula. **Ask: what is this research FOR, and what would have to be true for it to actually work?**

**Alternative research route (apply only where the item’s MS supports it):** secondary research can be quicker or easier to collate (for example, Tata’s car-manufacturer websites/reports). It may help when broad background is needed promptly, but its usefulness still depends on relevance and currency. Do not assume that it is free, current, customer-specific, or appropriate to every one of these cases.

**The scheme's own NOF chains (May–June 2021 P1, 3(e))** — *the source PDF is character-fragmented, and the wording below is repaired by spacing only, per rule 124:* *"Questionnaires will let NOF approach potential customers face to face to see what will attract them to visit the farm"* → *"NOF can ask specific questions which will give it detailed feedback that it can use to help attract more customers to its farm"* → AO4 *"NOF may not get all of the information it requires or people may not want to fill in the questionnaires whilst they are shopping so it might not be very helpful /limited feedback"*; against the internet: *"NOF can view a large selection of websites to look at other farms and gather information"* → *"It will be less time consuming /costly as it can see what other farms offer already and compare it to its own to see if it can improve based on this information"* → AO4 **"NOF may end up spending a lot of time looking at feedback or outdated information which will not be very useful for it and it is not gaining specific feedback on its own farm"**. **Read the two AO4s together and the decision is a case-linked evaluative consideration: the questionnaire fails when the shopper will not stop to fill it in, and the internet fails when the feedback is not about the farm.** The scheme's own AO3 for the internet is the one candidates miss — **"less time consuming /costly"** is a *cost* argument, not an information one.

**The scheme's own TUI chains (May–June 2024 P2 3(e)):** internet — *"TUI could use the internet to view a wide variety of customers comments made about their experiences and service received whilst on holiday in Greece"* → *"By using the internet it does reduce the time spent researching and is less costly than other methods available"* → AO4 **"TUI could spend a great deal of time looking at information which is not relevant to them or is out of date, or even places they do not offer holidays to"**; questionnaire — *"Customers can take their time completing the form giving details of the experiences/services they received whilst taking part in a day trip around Rome"* → *"Thus giving TUI detailed information of their trip and possible ways to improve it"* → AO4 **"not all customers may want to complete the questionnaires so there may be less data to use"**. **The internet's AO4 names three separate ways it wastes the researcher's time — irrelevant, out of date, and destinations TUI does not sell — and the questionnaire's names one way it starves the sample. Say which risk you are accepting.**


### TUI — May–June 2024 P2 Q3(e): internet vs customer questionnaire

**QP wording:** *"TUI constantly checks for customer feedback and comments on its holidays, including hotel stays and cruises. It is considering the following two options to gain the most up-to-date views: Option 1: using the internet. Option 2: asking customers to complete a questionnaire. (e) Justify which one of these two options TUI should use."* (printed p.16; identifier P75843A01624.)

**Matched MS (p.16, 3/3/3):** internet comments may cover Greek holiday experiences and be faster/less costly to research; questionnaire responses may give detail about services on a Rome day trip and possible improvements. Risks: internet material may be irrelevant, outdated or about destinations TUI does not sell; some customers may not complete questionnaires, reducing data. Level 3 requires detailed application, analysis and clear judgement supported by relevant evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would use questionnaires when TUI needs the most up-to-date, trip-specific feedback from its own customers.** A customer can describe a hotel stay, cruise or day trip in detail, which may help TUI identify a service to improve. Internet comments are quicker and less costly to review and may provide a wide variety of views, but they could be old, irrelevant or relate to places TUI does not offer. The questionnaire has a different risk: some customers may not respond, leaving TUI with less data. I would choose questionnaires if TUI can collect responses while the experience is fresh and obtain enough replies; if it needs broad feedback quickly across many destinations, internet research may be more efficient, provided it filters out irrelevant or outdated comments. No response-rate or research-volume data is supplied.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Gain the most up-to-date customer views on TUI holidays, stays, cruises and experiences. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Internet can surface varied holiday comments quickly and cheaply; questionnaires can gather detailed direct feedback on a specific experience. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Internet may be stale/irrelevant/out-of-destination; questionnaire may yield fewer responses. |
| **Evaluation** | Choose between breadth/speed and current direct detail, based on response rate and filtering capacity. |

**Available exemplar/report:** local `2024-06_P2_TUI.md` has a Q2(f) pricing exemplar, not a Q3(e) scored response; no item-specific scored questionnaire exemplar is identified there.

**Alternative chain:** internet research may be preferable when TUI needs broad feedback promptly, if it can limit results to current comments about destinations/services it sells.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** the QP makes recency central. Do not equate “internet” with current data or “questionnaire” with a representative sample; both depend on relevance and response.

### TUI, May–June 2024 P2 Q2(f) remains in entry 197; its source-specific model and attached teaching table are recorded there.

---

## 206. MARKET RESEARCH — questionnaires versus focus groups

**Asked:** *"Option 1: questionnaires, easy for passengers to complete · Option 2: focus groups, allowing passengers to give their opinions face-to-face."* — **May–June 2020 P2, 2(f)**, Emirates
**Also asked:** *"Option 1: hand out questionnaires to customers that visit the shops · Option 2: hold focus group meetings with parents and observe children playing with the new toys."* — **June 2022 P1, 3(e)**, TBTS

> **Both methods are primary research — so the usual "primary beats secondary" argument does not apply here.** **The comparison is depth against coverage, and which of the two the business actually needs.**

**The AO2 anchors (Emirates):** questionnaires are **easy for passengers to complete**; focus groups **allow passengers to give their opinions face-to-face**.
**The AO2 anchors (TBTS):** questionnaires **get feedback directly from the customers who buy the toys on behalf of children**; focus groups **let TBTS observe children playing with the new range**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on TBTS)*
> **TBTS should hold the focus groups.** The decisive fact is **who the real user of the product is and who does the buying.** **A toy may be bought by a parent and used by a child, so asking only the purchaser may not reveal how the child responds.** A focus group lets TBTS "observe children playing with the new range of toys", which could provide evidence about how children use the product. A questionnaire can still provide parents' views and feedback from customers; the methods answer related but not identical questions. **However, "the responses from the children may not be valid as they are in a new setting and may find other things more interesting"** — children in a strange room behave differently from children at home, so **observed behaviour is not automatically real behaviour.** **And the questionnaire's weakness compounds the problem: "as TBTS have shops in busy locations like airports, people may not have the time to fill in the questionnaires or may rush it, therefore not giving them accurate feedback."** **I would still choose the focus groups, on the condition that the toy is taken home and observed there for a week — because a focus group that tells you what a child does in an unfamiliar room has replaced one uncertain answer with another.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The focus groups** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | The purchaser and user may differ; observing children could add evidence about how they respond to the toy |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Children **behave differently in a new setting**; questionnaires at airport shops **are rushed and inaccurate** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the toy is taken home and observed there** |

**On Emirates the balance tips the other way, and the reason is the population.** **Focus groups can only include "a certain number of passengers and therefore might not give a true cross section of all classes of passengers"** — and an airline needs to know about **first class and economy, long-haul and short-haul**. A small group may not represent that spread. So for Emirates: **questionnaires, on the condition that they are handed out to a genuinely mixed set of passengers across cabin classes** — with the scheme's warning that **"often they are not filled in correctly and do not give the information about flights needed"**, which is exactly what a badly designed form produces.

> ⭐ **One potentially useful comparison for this pair is depth versus breadth of feedback.** Which method is preferable depends on the information the business needs and how representative or reliable the responses are.

---

## 207. MARKET RESEARCH — questionnaires versus tasting the product

**Asked:** *"Option 1: use questionnaires to collect customer views on the new toppings · Option 2: offer free samples of new toppings and ask customers for their opinions."* — **June 2026 P1R, 3(e)**, Spud Bros

> **This pair is the newest in the qualification and the most literal: ask people what they think, or let them try it and then ask.** **The second option is still research — it is a taste test — which is why the scheme puts it in the research section.**

**The AO2 anchors:** questionnaires **allow people to provide quantitative and qualitative feedback on the new toppings**; free samples **may encourage more people to taste the food**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **Spud Bros should offer the free samples.** The decisive fact is **what is being researched: taste — and taste cannot be reported, it has to be experienced.** **A customer who has not eaten the new topping may find it harder to judge its taste in a questionnaire, so stated preferences may not match reactions after sampling.** **Free samples "encourage more people to taste the food", so the opinion collected is a reaction to the actual product rather than to a description of it.** **The questionnaire does one thing the samples cannot: it collects "quantitative and qualitative feedback", meaning the answers can be counted and the reasons behind them captured — which matters if the brothers need to compare three new toppings against each other.** **However, "many people do not have the time to fill in a questionnaire and the response rate may be low for Spud Bros"** — a questionnaire with too few responses decides nothing. **However, giving free samples "will be costly" and will "take time to record participants' responses", which for a small family business is money and hours it cannot easily spare.** **I would still choose the samples, on the condition that the customers are asked their view immediately after tasting, before they walk away — because the taste is the research, and the opinion only has value while it is fresh.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The free samples** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **Taste must be experienced, not described** → samples produce **real reactions, not guesses** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Questionnaires → **low response rate**; samples → **costly for a small business, and recording responses takes time** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the opinion is captured immediately after tasting** |

**Second acceptable chain:** a free sample **is also a promotion, not just research** → the customer tastes, likes, and buys → **so the cost of the research is partly recovered in sales on the day**, which no questionnaire can do.

> ⭐ **The transferable test: is the thing being researched something the customer can REPORT, or something they can only EXPERIENCE?** **Price sensitivity, brand awareness and past behaviour can be asked about. Taste, feel, ease of use and enjoyment have to be tried.** **Match the method to the senses.**

---

## 208. MEASURING SUCCESS — customer satisfaction versus profit

**Asked:** *"Option 1: customer satisfaction · Option 2: profit."* — **June 2025 P1R, 2(f)**, CCT

> **This is the only 9-mark question in the qualification about MEASURING SUCCESS rather than doing something.** **Note that both measures are legitimate — the question is which tells CCT more about whether the business is working.**

**The AO2 anchors:** customer satisfaction **will indicate how happy customers are with the level of the bicycle touring service**; a high level of profit **will show CCT they can provide the tours at a cost lower than the price they are selling them for**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **CCT should measure customer satisfaction.** The decisive fact is **what the business is: a bicycle touring company whose product IS the experience of the tour.** **Satisfaction measures the thing the customer actually bought** — how happy they were with the tour — and **for a tour business, the customer's own verdict is not a proxy for quality, it is the quality.** **It also predicts the future rather than reporting the past: a satisfied customer recommends the tours, which produces the bookings CCT has not made yet.** **Profit, by contrast, is a lagging measure.** It tells CCT **that the pricing has worked over a period**, but **"profit can be affected by short-term fluctuations in the market, making it difficult to measure success over a longer period of time"** — a good season of weather can flatter a profit figure while the tours themselves are being run badly. **However, "it is difficult to measure customer satisfaction accurately as it relies on customers leaving reviews and providing honest feedback of their experiences"** — the measure depends on the customer bothering, and satisfied people are the least likely to bother. **I would still choose customer satisfaction, on the condition that the feedback is collected from every tour rather than waiting for reviews — because the customers who never write a review are as much part of the verdict as the ones who do.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **Customer satisfaction** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **The experience IS the product** → satisfaction measures what was bought, **and predicts future bookings** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Satisfaction → **hard to measure; relies on reviews and honesty**; profit → **distorted by short-term market fluctuations** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided feedback comes from every tour, not only from reviews** |

**Second acceptable chain:** profit **is the measure the owner can act on directly**, because it is a number in the accounts rather than an opinion → **but a tour business that is profitable this season and disliked by its customers will not be profitable next season.**

> ⚠️ **The profit caveat in this scheme — "affected by short-term fluctuations in the market" — is a genuinely unusual argument and worth remembering.** **It says profit is not wrong, it is UNRELIABLE as a long-term measure** — which is a more sophisticated point than "profit matters less than customers", and it is where the AO4 marks are.

---

## ✅ THE RESEARCH AND SUCCESS-MEASUREMENT CLUSTER — COMPLETE

**4 entries covering 9 blocks.** Nine blocks in the tier are research or success measurement, and they reduce to **four decisions**:

| Decision | Blocks | Entry |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary vs secondary research** | **5** — Lavender · Tata · NOF · TUI · Aude Rose | **205** |
| **Questionnaires vs focus groups** | **2** — Emirates · TBTS | **206** |
| **Questionnaires vs tasting the product** | **1** — Spud Bros | **207** |
| **Customer satisfaction vs profit** | **1** — CCT | **208** |

> ### ⭐ THE THREE QUESTIONS THAT DECIDE ANY RESEARCH CHOICE
> **1. WHO can answer this?** — Not "who is available". **Aude Rose needs its own customers, NOF needs people who have never visited, TBTS needs the child rather than the parent.** **If the wrong people are asked, the data is worthless however well it is collected.**
> **2. Can the customer REPORT it, or must they EXPERIENCE it?** — Price, brand and past behaviour can be asked about. **Taste, feel and enjoyment have to be tried.**
> **3. Do you need DEPTH on a few, or a CROSS-SECTION of many?** — **How something will be used → depth. What a whole customer base thinks → cross-section.**

---
# BATCH 7e — 9-MARK JUSTIFY, PART 5 — JOB DESIGN, MOTIVATION AND CONTRACTS

**9 entries covering 14 blocks — every job design, motivation and contract-type Justify question in the qualification.**

> ### ⭐ THE DOMINANT PAIR IN THIS CLUSTER: A BONUS VERSUS JOB ENRICHMENT
> **It appears four times — Backyard Shoez, Starbucks, IKEA and Huawei — across four series.** And the scheme's two counter-arguments are *identical in all four*:
>
> ⭐ **The bonus fails because not everyone can win it, or not everyone wants it:** *"employees may not be interested in a bonus as it requires more work and effort"* · *"not all employees are interested in bonus payments, as many are more interested in a good work-life balance"* · *"employees may not be interested in bonus payments if their bonus is based on everyone who works in the factory, as some employees may not work as hard as others"* · *"if an employee cannot continually achieve the highest sales then they may become demotivated."*
> ⭐ **Job enrichment fails because it suits some people and not others:** *"not all employees are motivated by non-financial methods such as job enrichment, they may prefer to have additional remuneration"* · *"some employees may resent not being chosen for job enrichment, and therefore may become demotivated."*
>
> **So the deciding question is never "which motivates people more" — it is WHOSE REWARD IS IT, and does it suit THIS workforce?** **That is what the case facts are for.**

---

## 209. MOTIVATION — a bonus versus job enrichment

**Asked four times:**
- *"Option 1: bonuses for whoever achieves the highest shoe sales in a month · Option 2: job enrichment, giving employees greater responsibilities."* — **May–June 2019 P1, 2(f)**, Backyard Shoez
- *"Option 1: bonus payments if the coffee shop increases its sales · Option 2: non-financial benefits such as job enrichment, so employees gain more skills and knowledge."* — **June 2025 P2, 2(f)**, Starbucks
- *"Option 1: the opportunity of earning a bonus to encourage carpenters to make more tables and chairs · Option 2: job enrichment, such as training present employees so they can work in the packing department."* — **May 2023 P2, 2(f)**, IKEA
- *"Option 1: financial methods such as bonus payments and piece rates · Option 2: non-financial benefits such as job enrichment."* — **Nov 2021 P2, 2(f)**, Huawei

> **Four papers, one decision, and the case facts point in genuinely different directions. This is the entry to read if you want to see how the same pair produces opposite answers.**

**The AO2 anchors (IKEA):** the bonus **encourages carpenters to make more tables and chairs**; job enrichment means **training present employees so they are able to work in the packing department**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(IKEA, May 2023 P2, 3(e); constructed)*
> **I would choose the bonus if IKEA’s factory employees value extra pay and can meet the production targets.** The MS says the opportunity to earn a bonus may encourage carpenters to make more tables and chairs, and that an added salary may help retain them. Job enrichment offers a different benefit: training current employees to work in the packing department may build skills, make them feel valued and support promotion to assistant manager. However, a bonus requires extra effort and may not appeal to employees who do not want that trade-off; job enrichment will not motivate everyone, since some prefer additional pay. I would choose the bonus if employees value the money and can influence the target; if skill development and progression matter more, job enrichment may better motivate them. The QP does not give employee preferences or bonus terms.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Motivate IKEA factory employees; the QP does not state a separate output target. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Bonus may encourage carpenters to make tables/chairs and add to salary; enrichment trains employees for packing and may support skills, value and promotion. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Bonus requires extra effort; some employees prefer extra pay to non-financial enrichment. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare employee preferences and whether targets are attainable/influenceable; bonus terms are not given. |

**Available exemplar/report:** `2023-05_Series_Irsi_AudeRose_IKEA_MM.md` records a 5/9 IKEA P2 Q3(e) candidate response and examiner commentary. The score applies to that script, not this constructed answer.

**Alternative chain:** enrichment may be preferable if IKEA can give employees useful packing skills and a credible route to promotion; the MS notes that some employees may instead prefer additional pay.

> ⭐ **IKEA takeaway:** answer the motivation question, not an assumed factory output problem. The QP/MS does not specify target design or employee preferences.

### Starbucks — June 2025 P2 Q2(f): financial vs non-financial methods for retention

**QP wording:** *"Starbucks wants to ensure that its employees remain with the business. To do this it is considering the following two methods: Option 1: financial methods. Option 2: non-financial methods. (f) Justify which one of these two methods Starbucks should choose."* (printed p.10; identifier P78796A01020.)

**MS (p.12, 3/3/3):** financial methods include a bonus if the coffee shop increases sales; income may encourage employees to stay. Job enrichment may provide skills/knowledge and a possible promotion route to assistant manager, supporting loyalty. Risks: some employees prefer work–life balance/non-financial rewards; others want extra pay to meet bills. Level 3 requires detailed application, logical analysis and a thorough, relevant judgement.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose non-financial methods for Starbucks if employees value the development and progression that job enrichment can offer.** Enrichment may give coffee-shop employees new skills and knowledge, help them feel valued and create a route towards assistant manager, which the MS links to loyalty and staying. A bonus tied to increased coffee-shop sales also has a plausible retention benefit because employees may value extra income. However, not everyone wants a sales-linked bonus; some prefer work–life balance and other non-financial incentives. Conversely, not all employees value enrichment, and some need additional pay to meet bills. I would favour enrichment where Starbucks can provide meaningful development and a progression route; if employees primarily value income and can influence sales, financial rewards may work better. The QP gives no survey of employee preferences or bonus-target details.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Retain Starbucks employees—not simply raise sales or profit. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Bonus linked to coffee-shop sales may give valued income; enrichment may build skills, value and progression to assistant manager. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Some prefer work–life balance/non-financial incentives; others prefer added pay to meet bills. |
| **Evaluation** | Judge by employee preferences and whether development or the sales-linked reward is meaningful. |

**Available exemplar/report:** `2025-06_Series_CCT_Starbucks_Rabanne.md` contains a 7/9 response for this exact item and examiner comment; it is evidence about that script, not a mandatory template.

**Alternative chain:** financial methods may work better if employees value additional income and consider the sales target attainable; retention still depends on whether the bonus is meaningful to them.

> ⭐ **Starbucks takeaway:** the examiner report warns against replacing the retention objective with revenue/profit. Sales is an MS mechanism, not the question’s objective.

### Huawei — November 2021 P2 Q2(f): financial vs non-financial motivation to retain employees

**QP wording:** *"Huawei use a range of motivational methods to ensure its employees remain with them and do not leave. It has to make a decision on which of the following two options it will use. It is considering two methods: Option 1: financial. Option 2: non-financial. (f) Justify which one of these two options Huawei should choose."* (printed p.10; identifier P66475RA01020.)

**MS (p.12, 3/3/3):** bonuses/piece rates may keep employees productive and encourage them to stay. Job enrichment may help employees enjoy work, gain skills and feel valued, with a possible route to supervisor of staff assembling tablets or watches. Risks: tablet assemblers may miss targets and receive no extra wage; some employees may prefer smartphone production and additional wages to pay bills. Level 3 requires detailed application, logical analysis and thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose job enrichment for Huawei’s retention objective if employees value developing into supervisory work.** The MS connects enrichment to skills and feeling valued, and gives the example of promotion to supervisor of staff assembling tablets or watches. Financial methods such as bonus payments or piece rates may also encourage employees to stay by adding pay and supporting productivity. But tablet assemblers may fail to meet targets and then receive no extra wage; some staff may prefer to keep producing smartphones and receive extra money to pay bills rather than take on enriched work. I would favour enrichment where employees want the new responsibility and a credible promotion path; if they value income and prefer their current production role, financial rewards may be more persuasive. The QP gives no employee preference, target or bonus details.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Keep Huawei employees from leaving. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Bonuses/piece rates may reward productive work and retention; enrichment may build skills/value and a route to supervising tablet/watch assembly. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Employees may miss bonus targets; some prefer smartphone production and additional wages to enrichment. |
| **Evaluation** | Judge against employee preferences, target attainability and whether the promotion path is credible. |

**Available examiner evidence:** the local advisory report records the Principal Examiner’s warning that candidates often described options without justifying them. No item-specific scored candidate script is mapped locally.

**Alternative chain:** financial rewards may best support retention if employees prefer pay and Huawei sets targets they can influence; if targets are missed, the MS identifies a risk that the reward has no effect.

> ⭐ **Huawei takeaway:** compare the tablet/watch development path with extra wages for smartphone-production employees. Do not use a cost-saving or productivity-only rationale as the judgement.

**Backyard Shoez runs the other way — and the reason is that the bonus is a COMPETITION.** The scheme says it is for *"the person who has achieved the highest shoe sales"*, and warns that **"if an employee cannot continually achieve the highest sales then they may become demotivated and this could have the opposite effect."** **A prize that only one person can win demotivates everyone else by design.** **So for Backyard Shoez: job enrichment, provided the extra responsibility is offered to everyone rather than to a chosen few** — because the scheme's objection to enrichment is *"some employees may resent not being chosen."* **Both options in this pair fail in the same way — by excluding people — and the fix is the same.**

The Huawei and Starbucks items have different stated objectives and MS examples; see the separate source-specific sections below. Do not transfer IKEA’s target/output reasoning to them.

> ⭐ **Item-specific reminder:** answer the named objective and use only the relevant case’s MS examples. The four cases in this entry do not share a universal motivation mechanism or preferred choice.

---

## 210. JOB DESIGN — job rotation, job enrichment and added tasks

**Three distinct QP prompts and objectives (keep the options in their printed order):**
- **Yellow, May–June 2019 P2R, 3(e):** *"Yellow is considering using non-financial rewards to help retain its employees. It is considering two options: Option 1: job enrichment. Option 2: job rotation. (e) Justify which one of these two options Yellow should use."*
- **NEXT, May–June 2021 P2, 3(e):** *"NEXT understands that some employees are motivated by non-financial methods and these may help with employee retention. To do this NEXT is considering the following two options: Option 1: job rotation. Option 2: job enrichment. (e) Justify which one of these two options NEXT should choose."*
- **M&M, May 2023 P2R, 3(e):** *"M&M wants to retain the employees working in its business. To do this it is considering the following options. Option 1: job rotation. Option 2: bonus. (e) Justify which one of these two options M&M should use."* Reviewed separately below.

> These questions share job-design terms, but their objectives and MS reasoning are case-specific. The excerpts below are MS illustrations from the named papers, not universal strengths, weaknesses or marking rules.

| Method | MS illustration in the named item | Item-specific caveat |
|---|---|---|
| **Adding tasks** | Yellow (2019 P2R): additional tasks such as supervising newly recruited employees | Yellow’s MS says the employee may later demand more pay. |
| **Job rotation** | NEXT (2021 P2): move from stock room to shop floor; M&M (2023 P2R) has a separate production-line context | NEXT’s MS notes the employee may seek other jobs; M&M’s MS notes rotation may not increase wages. |
| **Job enrichment** | Yellow (2019 P2R): greater responsibility; NEXT (2021 P2): use display skills | The MS caveats differ: Yellow may face a pay demand; NEXT may incur time/training costs. |

**MS illustrations (not QP wording):** For NEXT, the MS illustrates rotation with moving from the stock room to the shop floor; it says those skills may be used within NEXT. It illustrates enrichment with employees using display skills in the furniture and children’s-clothes windows. The MS says rotation may prompt staff to seek other jobs, while enrichment may require time and training. For Yellow, the MS illustrates enrichment with added tasks such as supervising new recruits, and rotation between customer-facing store work and the stock room; it links enrichment to appreciation/commitment and rotation to reduced boredom and busy-period flexibility.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(May–June 2019 P2R, 3(e); constructed)*
> **Yellow should use job enrichment to support its aim of retaining employees.** Giving an employee additional tasks such as supervising new recruits may help them gain experience and feel appreciated, which the MS links to greater commitment to Yellow. Job rotation could also make work less repetitive by moving staff between customers in the store and stock work, and it may help managers cover busy periods. However, Yellow’s MS warns that an enriched employee may later demand more pay, while rotation takes time and training and the employee may use the new skills to seek another job. On balance, enrichment is more closely linked in this MS to feeling appreciated and staying with the business, provided Yellow can recognise the added responsibility fairly. If Yellow’s immediate need is flexible cover at busy times rather than retention, rotation may be preferable.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Yellow wants to retain employees, not simply cover busy periods. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Added supervisory tasks may build experience and appreciation, supporting commitment; rotation can relieve boredom and provide cover. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Enrichment may prompt a pay demand; rotation needs training/time and may give employees skills they use to leave. |
| **Evaluation** | Weigh retention against the cost of added responsibility; rotation remains plausible if operational cover is the priority. |

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(May–June 2021 P2, 3(e); constructed)*
> **I would choose job rotation for NEXT’s stated aim of employee retention, if the new roles create development opportunities within the business.** Moving from the stock room to the shop floor may give employees additional skills and knowledge they can use at NEXT, as the MS illustrates. Job enrichment also has a case-specific benefit: staff may use their display skills in the windows for furniture and children’s clothes. However, NEXT’s MS notes that rotation experiences may lead employees to seek other jobs; enrichment has a different cost, taking time and training, and its display skills may also support applications elsewhere. Neither method guarantees retention. I would choose rotation if NEXT can use the acquired skills in its own roles and make that pathway visible; if it cannot, the training cost of enrichment or the risk of either method making employees more employable elsewhere may outweigh the retention benefit.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | NEXT is considering non-financial methods to help retain employees. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Rotation offers experience from stock room to shop floor and skills usable within NEXT; enrichment uses employees’ display skills. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Rotation may lead employees to seek other jobs; enrichment may require additional time and training. |
| **Evaluation** | The choice depends on whether NEXT can offer internal use/progression for the new skills; do not treat either method as automatically retaining staff. |

**Alternative for NEXT:** job enrichment may be preferable if NEXT can use employees’ window-display skills and give them worthwhile responsibility; weigh that against the MS’s time/training cost and possibility that employees use the skills in applications elsewhere.

**M&M (May 2023 P2R, 3(e)) has its own completed source-specific model below; its retention objective and production-line examples are distinct from Yellow and NEXT.**

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway for these reviewed blocks:** start from the stated objective. Yellow, NEXT and M&M ask about retention, but their options and MS mechanisms differ. Use each item’s own analysis and do not transfer conclusions across cases.


### M&M — May 2023 P2R Q3(e): job rotation vs bonus for retention

**QP wording:** *"M&M wants to retain the employees working in its business. To do this it is considering the following options: Option 1: job rotation. Option 2: bonus. (e) Justify which one of these two options M&M should use."* (printed p.15; identifier P72954A01520.)

**MS (p.20, 3/3/3):** job rotation moves employees on/off the production line between manufacturing and designing vehicle parts, involving them across production; new experience/skills may make them more likely to stay. A bonus tied to car-manufacturing targets may make employees feel valued in the car road-testing section and encourage harder work. Risks: rotation may not increase wages; a factory-wide bonus may be unpopular if effort varies among employees. Level 3 requires detailed contextual application, logical analysis and clear judgement with thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose job rotation for M&M’s stated aim of retaining employees if staff value learning different parts of production.** Moving between manufacturing and designing vehicle parts may give employees new experience and skills, which the MS links to staying rather than looking for another job. A bonus tied to car-manufacturing targets may also make workers feel valued and encourage greater effort, including in the road-testing section. However, rotation may not increase wages, while a factory-wide bonus may seem unfair if employees believe others contribute less. I would choose rotation if development is valued and roles can be rotated safely; if employees mainly want additional pay and M&M can set transparent, controllable targets, a bonus may be more effective. The QP/MS does not say what employees prefer or how the bonus is calculated.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Retain M&M employees. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Rotation across manufacturing/design may build experience and skills; target-linked bonus may make employees feel valued and encourage effort. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Rotation may not raise wages; factory-wide bonus may be viewed as unfair if effort differs. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare employee preferences and perceived fairness; bonus terms and skill-development value are not specified in QP. |

**Available exemplar/report:** `2023-05_Series_Irsi_AudeRose_IKEA_MM.md` maps two candidate responses for this exact item: 3/9 (Level 1) and 9/9 (Level 3), with the high-scoring script and commentary transcribed. The examiner’s evaluative consideration in the 9/9 includes bonus size and how the bonus system works; it is evidence from that response, not a required phrase.

**Alternative chain:** a bonus may be the better retention choice if employees value pay and the target is fair and under their influence; avoid assuming a group-wide bonus motivates every worker equally.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** answer retention, not simply output. Rotation’s experience/skills and bonus’s pay/recognition are distinct routes; compare the wage and fairness risks rather than transferring the NEXT job-rotation model.

---

## 211. MOTIVATION — extra holiday versus a bonus

**QP wording:** *"Nando’s plans to offer restaurant managers a reward to try to retain them in the business. Nando’s is considering two types of reward: Option 1: offer the managers an extra five days’ paid holiday a year. Option 2: pay a bonus to the managers whose restaurants achieve the highest levels of profit. (e) Justify which one of these two options Nando’s should choose to retain managers."* — **June 2026 P2, 3(e)**.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording):** the MS links the extra holiday to work–life balance and possible retention; the bonus to attempts to sell more chicken, salad and wraps and retention of financially motivated managers. It notes that managers may already have enough holiday, or may doubt their restaurant can achieve the highest profit levels and therefore not be persuaded to stay.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose the extra five days’ paid holiday to help Nando’s retain restaurant managers.** The benefit is available to the managers as a retention reward and may improve work–life balance, which the MS identifies as a possible reason for them to stay. A bonus could also help retention: managers who value financial rewards may try to sell more chicken, salad and wraps. However, the bonus depends on their restaurant achieving the highest levels of profit, and a manager who doubts that target is attainable may not see it as a reason to remain. The holiday is not certain to retain managers either; the MS notes that some may already have enough holiday. On balance, I would offer the additional paid days if managers value time away from work; if the managers are more responsive to financial rewards and can influence their restaurant’s profit result, the bonus could be more effective. The QP and MS do not say that profit must grow, that only one manager can qualify, or how the bonus is calculated.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Choice / objective** | Additional paid holiday as the retention reward; alternative choice depends on what the Nando’s managers value. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Holiday may improve work–life balance and support staying; a bonus may motivate sales efforts and appeal to financially motivated managers. These are possible effects, not guaranteed results. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Holiday may be insufficient if managers already have enough time off; a bonus may not retain a manager who doubts the restaurant can achieve the stated highest-profit condition. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Compare managers’ preferences and whether they can influence the restaurant’s result. Do not convert “highest levels of profit” into “profit growth,” assume only one winner, or call the bonus self-funding without evidence about its terms and cost. |

**Alternative chain:** if the managers place greater value on financial rewards and regard the profit condition as achievable, the bonus may support retention by rewarding sales efforts; if not, extra holiday may be the more credible benefit. This is conditional on manager preferences and the bonus criteria, which the QP does not specify.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** answer the stated retention objective, and evaluate the actual reward conditions. “Highest profit” is not the same as profit growth, and the question does not establish a winner count, bonus formula or automatic link between bonus expense and profit.

---

## 212. MOTIVATION — commission versus a higher hourly rate

**Asked:** *"Option 1: commission to encourage teachers to find new pupils · Option 2: an increased hourly rate to encourage teachers to work longer hours."* — **May–June 2020 P1, 2(f)**, TADS

> **Both options are financial — so this is not financial against non-financial. It is PAY FOR FINDING WORK against PAY FOR DOING WORK**, and that difference decides it.

**The AO2 anchors:** commission **would encourage its teachers to find new pupils who want to learn music**; a higher hourly rate **would encourage music teachers to work for longer hours**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **TADS should use the commission.** The decisive fact is **what the business actually needs: a drumming school with too few pupils has a demand problem, not an hours problem.** **Commission pays for finding new pupils, which is the thing the school is short of** — and paying for a result means **TADS only pays out once a pupil has arrived and started paying fees.** **An increased hourly rate pays for time, whether or not there is any work for the teacher to do.** **That direction matters, because the scheme's own objection to the hourly rate is that "if TADS cannot increase the number of pupils it may not be able to retain all of the teachers it currently employs"** — **paying more per hour without more pupils simply raises the cost of serving the pupils it already has.** **However, "the commission is a one-off payment and once paid the teachers would only receive their hourly rate, which may not be sufficient to continue to be employed by TADS"** — so commission brings a teacher a pupil but does not make them stay. **I would still choose the commission, on the condition that it is paid on pupils who actually stay for a term — because a payment on signatures rather than on retained pupils pays teachers to recruit people who will not come back.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The commission** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | The problem is **too few pupils, not too few hours** → pay for the result the business lacks |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Commission → **one-off; the teacher's ongoing rate is unchanged**; hourly rate → **costs more with no more pupils, and may not be affordable** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided it is paid on pupils who stay, not on sign-ups** |

**Second acceptable chain:** a higher hourly rate **rewards the teachers who are already good and therefore keeps them**, which matters in a school whose product is the quality of its teaching → **but it rewards loyalty rather than growth, and the scheme is clear that the enrolment problem is the one that would cost TADS its teachers.**

> ⭐ **The decisive question: what is the business short of — DEMAND or CAPACITY?** **Short of customers → pay for bringing them in (commission).** **Short of output or hours → pay for time (a higher rate).** **TADS has an empty class, not a tired teacher.**

---

## 213. COSTS — reducing wages by using part-time staff versus reducing commission

**Asked:** *"Option 1: employ part-time tour guides instead of full-time tour guides · Option 2: reduce the rate of commission paid to its sales team."* — **Nov 2025 P1, 3(e)**, Ocean Vibes

> **Read what this question is: the stem asks which cost to cut, not which reward to give.** **Both options save money — so the answer depends entirely on which saving damages the business less.**

**The AO2 anchors:** part-time guides **reduce the wages of the wildlife tour guides**; reducing commission from 5% **lowers the total variable costs of the boat tours**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **Ocean Vibes should reduce the commission rather than move to part-time guides.** The decisive fact is **who delivers the product: the tour guides ARE the dolphin and bird tours.** **A part-time guide is one who is not there on the day the guests wanted** — and the scheme warns that **"it is possible that part-time employees are less committed to the business if they prefer full time work, which may lead to tour guides leaving Ocean Vibes for alternative employment."** **The saving is real but it is bought by putting the touring at risk, and the tours are the entire revenue.** **Commission is different in one important way: it is a variable cost, paid only when the sales team has actually sold something** — so the saving follows the sales rather than preceding them. **However, that is also exactly why it is dangerous: "the sales team may be less motivated to work, which may lead to a reduction in the revenue and the profitability of boat tours for Ocean Vibes."** **Cutting the reward of the people who bring in money reduces the money coming in.** **I would still cut the commission, on the condition that the rate is reduced by a stated amount rather than tied to a sales target the team cannot influence — because a demotivated sales team costs more in lost bookings than the commission ever saved.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **Reduce the commission** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **The guides ARE the tours** → part-time risks the product and the staff | 
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Part-time guides → **less committed, likely to leave**; commission cut → **sales team demotivated → revenue falls** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the cut is a stated amount, not a target the team cannot influence** |
