**Second acceptable chain:** wage cost is **fixed and paid whether or not a single tour runs** → in a season with bad weather, part-time guides are the difference between a loss and a surplus → **but saving a fixed cost by removing the people who deliver the service is a saving taken from the product itself.**

> ⚠️ **This question is a cost-reduction question wearing a motivation question's clothes. Identify which one you have before writing** — otherwise you will argue about motivation theory when the examiner is asking which cut is survivable. **The scheme's own framing gives it away: both options are phrased as cost reductions, not rewards.**

---

## 214. CONTRACTS — full-time versus part-time staff

**QP wording:** *"Yellow needs to recruit more sales assistants for its stores. It is considering two options: Option 1: employ only full-time staff. Option 2: employ only part-time staff. (f) Justify which one of these two options Yellow should use."* — **May–June 2019 P2R, 2(f)**.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording):** full-time staffing means fewer employees in each store and may provide consistency, commitment and loyalty. Part-time staffing offers flexibility in hours and can cover longer shop hours or busy periods. The MS weighs fixed pay regardless of demand against potentially higher recruitment costs if part-time staff are less committed.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would use part-time staff for Yellow’s sales-assistant vacancies if the stores need extra cover at busy times or across longer opening hours.** Part-time hours could let Yellow match the number of sales assistants on duty to customer demand, whereas full-time staff may provide more consistency and loyalty and require fewer employees in each store. The MS’s key risk for full-time staff is that their wages are paid regardless of demand, so the store may carry labour costs during quieter periods. Part-time staffing has a counter-risk: if those employees are less committed, Yellow may face higher recruitment costs. Since the QP gives no sales pattern or wage figures, the best choice depends on whether flexible cover saves enough labour cost to outweigh possible staff turnover. I would choose part-time staff for variable or extended-hours demand, but full-time staff may be preferable where consistent staffing and commitment matter more.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Recruit sales assistants for Yellow’s stores; compare staffing flexibility with consistency and commitment. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Part-time hours can cover busy periods/longer shop hours; full-time staffing involves fewer employees per store and may improve continuity. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Full-time wages may be paid irrespective of demand; part-time staff may be less committed and cost more to recruit. |
| **Possible evaluation** | The QP gives no demand pattern or costs. Choose according to actual store-hour coverage and the cost of turnover; do not invent weekend peaks or assume part-time is automatically cheaper. |

**Alternative chain:** if Yellow’s shops need reliable continuity more than variable-hour cover, full-time staff may be better because the MS identifies consistency, commitment and loyalty as potential strengths. That choice still needs to weigh wages paid in periods of lower demand.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** keep the question’s objective—recruiting sales assistants—distinct from a retention question. Use the MS’s flexible-hours and commitment illustrations, then evaluate costs against Yellow’s actual demand, which the QP does not quantify.

---

## 215. CONTRACTS — permanent versus temporary contract

**Asked:** *"Option 1: offer the new chef a permanent contract · Option 2: offer the new chef a temporary contract."* — **May–June 2022 P1R, 3(e)**, Can Culleretes

**The AO2 anchors:** a permanent contract means **the chef will be more committed to the restaurant long term**; a temporary contract lets them **wait to see if the desserts are popular before hiring on a permanent basis**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **Can Culleretes should offer the temporary contract.** The decisive fact is **what the business is testing and what a mistake would cost it: a dessert chef has been hired to produce a new dessert range, and nobody yet knows whether customers will want it.** **A temporary contract lets the restaurant "wait to see if the desserts are popular or not before hiring on a permanent basis"** — so **the decision that cannot be undone is deferred until there is evidence for it.** **That matters more in a family business than in a chain: the scheme notes that "as Can Culleretes is a family run business they may be cautious of having someone come into the business on a permanent basis, especially if the new desserts do not meet customer expectations."** **In a restaurant run by the family that owns it, a permanent appointment is a personal commitment as well as a contract.** **However, "a skilled dessert chef may not like the uncertainty of a temporary contract and may choose to work elsewhere"** — and the best chefs are the ones with options. **I would still choose the temporary contract, on the condition that it states what would make it permanent — because a competent chef will accept a trial, and will not accept an open-ended one.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The temporary contract** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **The dessert range is untested** → defer the irreversible decision until there is evidence |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Temporary → **a skilled chef may go elsewhere**; permanent → **a commitment the family business may regret** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the contract states what makes it permanent** |

**Second acceptable chain:** a permanent contract **secures commitment from the chef, who then invests in the restaurant's menu rather than just executing it** → **but a family restaurant that has traded for generations does not need to gamble its payroll on an untested dessert.**

> ⭐ **The decisive question: can the decision be UNDONE?** **Untested product, uncertain demand, a small business → temporary. Proven need, scarce skill, a role the business cannot function without → permanent.** **A useful judgement explains what would make one option preferable in this case; no fixed condition clause is required.**

---

## 216. CONTRACTS — part-time working versus job share

**QP wording:** *"Cadbury wants to retain its skilled employees. To do this it is looking to offer them different ways of working. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: allowing employees to work part-time. Option 2: allowing employees to job share. (f) Justify which one of these two options Cadbury should choose."* — **May–June 2022 P2R, 2(f)**.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording; MS PDF p.14):** part-time work offers employee flexibility and may help meet seasonal demand such as Christmas. A job share may cover design of chocolate wrappers when one employee is ill and gives Cadbury two people’s ideas and enthusiasm. The MS risks are possible added employment costs from legal benefits for part-time staff and the need for job sharers to work as a team and liaise effectively.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose job sharing if it is the arrangement Cadbury’s skilled employees prefer, because the question’s objective is to retain them.** Sharing a role may make the working arrangement more manageable for employees while keeping their skills available to Cadbury; the MS also illustrates that if one person is absent, the other may cover the design of chocolate wrappers, and that two people can contribute ideas and enthusiasm. Part-time work offers flexibility too and could help Cadbury respond to seasonal demand such as Christmas. However, part-time arrangements may bring extra employment costs, while a job share only works well if the two people can coordinate and communicate. I would favour job sharing where the employees value shared hours and can work together; if individual flexibility is more important to them, part-time work may better support retention. The QP gives no employee-preference or cost data, so neither arrangement is guaranteed to retain staff.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Retain Cadbury’s skilled employees; do not let the coverage benefit replace the retention question. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Part-time work may offer flexibility and help with Christmas demand; job share may maintain wrapper-design cover during absence and combine two employees’ ideas. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Part-time may increase employment costs; job share requires effective teamwork and liaison. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Judge by employee preferences and ability to coordinate; the QP gives no evidence to make either retention effect certain. |

**Alternative chain:** part-time work may be the better retention choice if the skilled employees value individual flexibility. The MS also connects it to seasonal demand, but that operational benefit alone does not prove that employees will stay.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** answer Cadbury’s stated retention objective. Use the MS’s cover and seasonal-demand examples as supporting business effects, but evaluate how each arrangement may suit employees and Cadbury; do not treat absence cover as the whole decision.

---

## 217. CONTRACTS — on-call staff versus student staff

**QP wording:** *"There are times during the year when Jimmy Choo needs additional employees to manufacture its products. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: casual employees. Option 2: part-time employees. (f) Justify which one of these two options Jimmy Choo should choose."* — **May–June 2024 P2R, 2(f)** (printed p.11; identifier P75844A01120).

> **Both options give flexibility, and neither guarantees commitment — so the question is which sort of unreliability is easier to live with.**

**The AO2 anchors:** some employees **like the option of being on call**; students **wanting to work during the summer holidays would be grateful for the opportunity**.

> **Earlier student-first illustration withdrawn.** Use the fully reviewed item-specific model below; the QP does not establish summer-only demand, guaranteed student availability or a need for a core supervisory workforce.

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | A conditional preference for casual cover during unpredictable shortages; part-time may fit planned seasonal needs. |
| **2. AO2 / AO3** | On-call staff may cover emergencies; students may want summer work and a temporary contract. |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Casual work may weaken commitment; part-time workers may not match needed hours or skills. |
| **4. Evaluation** | Let the timing and predictability of extra staffing determine the choice. |

**Alternative chain:** choose part-time workers if additional demand is planned well enough to agree hours and Jimmy Choo can confirm the workers have the required skills; this avoids treating every staffing need as an emergency.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** separate the MS’s possible summer-student fit from the QP’s broader “times during the year” need. Neither staff group is guaranteed to be available, committed or skilled.

**10 entries covering the last 21 blocks: training, production, operations, recruitment, communication, place, product and structure.**


### Reviewed item: Jimmy Choo, May–June 2024 P2R Q2(f) — casual vs part-time employees

**QP wording:** *"There are times during the year when Jimmy Choo needs additional employees to manufacture its products. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: casual employees. Option 2: part-time employees. (f) Justify which one of these two options Jimmy Choo should choose."* (printed p.11; identifier P75844A01120.)

**MS (p.13, 3/3/3):** casual staff may be on call for emergencies/shortages in handbag production and may value flexibility for family responsibilities; part-time students may want summer work, and Jimmy Choo can hire for a period without a permanent full-time commitment. Risks: casual employees have no guaranteed work and may lack commitment; part-time employees may not be available for required hours or possess needed skills. Level 3 requires detailed application, developed analysis and a clear judgement with thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose casual employees if Jimmy Choo’s additional staffing need is genuinely irregular or an emergency.** The QP says extra employees are needed at some times during the year, and the MS links casual staff to being on call when production is short. This could give the business flexibility without committing to a full-time permanent contract. Part-time workers, including students seeking summer work, may be grateful for a fixed period of employment and could support planned seasonal production. However, casual employees have no guarantee of work, which may weaken their commitment when called; part-time employees may not be available for the hours Jimmy Choo needs or have the required skills. I would favour casual staffing for unpredictable shortages, but part-time staff for a known busy period where hours and skills can be agreed in advance. The QP gives no schedule showing which kind of demand Jimmy Choo faces.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective/context** | Provide additional manufacturing employees at certain times of year. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | On-call cover can address emergencies/shortages; part-time summer workers may welcome temporary work, without a permanent full-time contract. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Casual staff lack guaranteed work/commitment; part-time staff may not match required hours or skills. |
| **Evaluation** | Irregular emergency demand favours on-call flexibility; predictable seasonal demand favours scheduled part-time hours, if skills match. |

**Available exemplar/report:** local `2024-06_P2R_Jimmy_Choo.md` records candidate responses at 8/9 (Level 3) for casual and 5/9 (Level 2) for casual; scripts are transcribed from printed images and flagged for recheck. This is item-specific evidence, not an answer formula.

**Alternative chain:** part-time students may be better for a planned summer peak because they want work in that period and Jimmy Choo can agree hours; it remains conditional on their skills and availability.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** the case is occasional additional staffing, not a general claim that students fit Jimmy Choo’s products or that casual staff are always unreliable. Match the contract to whether demand is planned or unpredictable.

## 218. TRAINING — on-the-job versus off-the-job, and induction

**Asked three times:** LEGO *(on-the-job keeps them producing vs off-site experts)* — **June 2022 P2, 3(e)** · Irsi Chocolatier *(on-the-job shows current techniques vs a college lecturer)* — **May 2023 P1, 3(e)** · Jimmy Choo *(induction in their own department vs off-site experts)* — **June 2024 P2R, 3(e)**

> **The pair changes name between papers — induction and off-site in one, on-the-job and off-the-job in another — but the decision is the same: learn it HERE, or learn it THERE.**

**The AO2 anchors (Irsi):** on-the-job **allows the new employee to see current chocolate-making techniques**; off-the-job **allows them to learn new techniques from lecturers at a college**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on Irsi Chocolatier)*
> **Irsi should train the new employee off-the-job.** The decisive fact is **the size of the business: the scheme says Irsi "only has 3 employees" — and with three people, there is nobody who can be spared to teach.** **On-the-job training assumes a skilled person standing beside the trainee, and in a three-person shop that person is also making the chocolates, serving the customers and taking the orders.** **The scheme's warning is precisely this: "there may not be someone who can dedicate the time to train the new employee to the standard required."** **Off-the-job training removes that problem entirely — the lecturer is not taken away from production because they were never part of it.** **It also brings in "new techniques from lecturers at a college", which a business this small cannot generate from inside.** **However, "this can be costly as the employee will have to attend an external course rather than learn on the job"** — and for a three-employee business, one wage plus a course fee with no production from that person is a real cost. **I would still choose off-the-job training, on the condition that the course is short and specific to chocolate-making — because a small business cannot absorb a general qualification, but it can absorb a two-week course that makes the new recruit productive.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **Off-the-job training** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **Three employees → nobody can be spared to teach** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Off-the-job → **costly, employee absent; course may not match the job**; on-the-job → **no one available to train to standard** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the course is short and specific to chocolate-making** |

**LEGO — May–June 2022 P2, 3(e): QP wording:** *"Ensuring that all employees are fully trained is important to LEGO. LEGO makes sure that all employees are fully trained and skilled to carry out the work. All employees have induction training when they start at LEGO. Additional training is undertaken by employees as they progress through the business. LEGO is considering the following two options to carry out this additional training. Option 1: on-the-job training. Option 2: off-the-job training. Justify which one of these two options LEGO should choose."*

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording; MS PDF p.17):** on-the-job training lets employees stay on the premises producing the Princess Leia toy and may build confidence with familiar staff; off-the-job training gives access to experts and new production methods, which the MS links to fewer errors and more toys per factory. Risks: on-the-job training can take time from skilled employees; off-site training may not teach the specific skills needed to design LEGO’s new Star Wars toys.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose on-the-job training for LEGO’s additional training when employees need skills specific to the work they will carry out, including designing new Star Wars toys.** Staff can remain at LEGO while training and continue producing the Princess Leia toy, and the MS suggests that learning from familiar employees may build confidence. Off-the-job training has a genuine advantage: experts can introduce methods not used in LEGO’s factories, and the MS links those skills to fewer errors and increased output. However, the MS warns that an external course may not develop the specific knowledge needed to design LEGO’s new Star Wars toys. On-the-job training also has a cost: experienced employees may spend time training instead of doing their own work efficiently. I would choose on-the-job training where the required skills are specific to LEGO’s products and an experienced employee is available; off-site training may be preferable when an expert method is needed and can be matched to the work. The QP/MS gives no information about trainer availability or course content, so factory count alone does not settle the choice.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Provide the additional training LEGO says employees need to be fully trained and skilled for their work. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | On-the-job keeps employees on-site producing the Princess Leia toy and may build confidence; off-site experts offer new methods that may reduce errors and increase output. |
| **AO4 comparison** | On-the-job takes skilled employees’ time; off-site may not provide the specific skills needed to design new Star Wars toys. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Match training to the skill required and compare trainer time with course content; the QP does not establish a trainer shortage or course suitability. |

**Alternative chain:** off-the-job training may be better if LEGO needs a method not currently available in its factories and the course is relevant to the employees’ work; its value would then depend on transferring that expertise to the design/production of new toys.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** decide based on training-skill fit and the stated learning objective, not the size of LEGO or an assumption that fifteen factories guarantee a trainer. The MS’s method and risk illustrations are not a universal training rule.

**The scheme's own Jimmy Choo chains (May–June 2024 P2R, 3(e)) — induction against off-site:** induction — *"If all employees have induction training it gives them the opportunity to become familiar with their working environment in the production of shoes"* → *"This training enables them to feel part of the business and have a better understanding of how the business works"* → AO4 **"However, induction training does not always benefit because they may not work in that specific department, they had their induction training in"**; off-site — *"By training employees off site they are trained by experts in the various areas such as well as making the handbags and shoes but also how to display them"* → *"Employees can gain up to date skills from experts, resulting in fewer errors when they commence making handbags"* → AO4 **"However, the cost of training an employee off-site can be expensive, and the training is not guaranteed that it will match the job"**. **This pair is not the same choice as LEGO's or Irsi's, and the scheme shows why: BOTH options here can miss the job.** Induction can be given "in that specific department" the employee never works in; an off-site course "is not guaranteed that it will match the job". **So a useful evaluation is about match, not cost — and the answer can weigh the relevant mismatch if it supports the decision.**


### Reviewed item: Jimmy Choo, May–June 2024 P2R Q3(e) — induction vs off-the-job training

**QP wording:** *"Jimmy Choo employs people in many countries to produce its shoes, handbags and perfume. To ensure that its new employees have the best start possible the following two training options are being considered: Option 1: induction training. Option 2: off-the-job training. (e) Justify which one of these two options Jimmy Choo should choose."* (printed p.15; identifier P75844A01520.)

**MS (p.17, 3/3/3):** induction familiarises employees with the shoe-production work environment and may help them feel part of the business/understand how it works. Off-site experts may teach skills in making handbags/shoes and displaying them; up-to-date skills may reduce errors when making handbags. Risks: induction may take place in a department the employee does not work in; off-site training may be costly and fail to match the job. Level 3 descriptor as above.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose induction training for Jimmy Choo’s new employees if it is delivered in the department where they will work.** The QP says the purpose is to give new employees the best start, and the MS links induction to familiarity with the shoe-production environment and understanding how the business works, which may help a new employee feel part of the company. Off-the-job training offers expert, up-to-date skills in making shoes or handbags and display, and may reduce errors. But that course could be expensive and may not match the employee’s actual job; induction also loses value if it is delivered in the wrong department. I would choose induction when it is role-specific and helps new staff settle in; I would choose off-the-job training when the new role requires expert skills that the course demonstrably teaches. The MS does not establish that every new employee has the same department or training need.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Give new employees a good start across Jimmy Choo’s international production operations. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Induction can familiarise with shoe-production surroundings and company operation; external experts can teach current product/display skills and reduce errors. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Induction can be misplaced to the wrong department; off-site training costs more and may not match the job. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare role fit and the skill gap; neither method is automatically best for all new employees. |

**Available exemplar/report:** local `2024-06_P2R_Jimmy_Choo.md` records Q3(e) examiner comments and Level 2 candidate outcomes at 4/9 and 6/9; candidate script bodies are not transcribed. The report emphasizes analysis and evaluation; its comment is not a mandatory conclusion rule.

**Alternative chain:** off-site training can be the better choice for employees who need specialised, current skills in handbags/shoes or display, if the course content matches their assigned duties and its cost is justified by fewer errors.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** compare the purpose (“best start”) with actual job-fit. Keep the two MS risks distinct; do not reuse the LEGO trainer-availability argument.

## 219. PRODUCTION — job, batch and flow production

**Three QP items use production-method choices; keep their case objectives and pairs distinct.**
- **Lavender, May–June 2019 P1R, 2(f):** local product range at each outlet vs flow production across all outlets (separate case).
- **Cadbury, May–June 2022 P2R, 3(e), exact QP wording:** *"Cadbury introduces new products on a regular basis, such as Dinky Deckers. The aim of this is to maintain customer loyalty by offering new products. Before releasing Dinky Deckers for sale to the public, Cadbury had to decide the method of production it would use. It considered the following two options: Option 1: batch production. Option 2: flow production. (e) Justify which one of these two options Cadbury should choose."*
- **Irsi Chocolatier, May 2023 P1, 2(f):** batch production vs job production for personalised lollipops (separate case).

The decision reasoning below is item-specific; no method has a universal advantage.

**The AO2 anchors (Irsi):** batch production **allows different flavours and shapes of chocolate lollipops**; job production **allows Irsi to give customers the option of personalised lollipops**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on Irsi Chocolatier)*
> **Irsi should use batch production.** The decisive fact is **what the customer is actually buying: a chocolate lollipop is an impulse purchase, taken from a display and paid for in seconds.** **Batch production gives "different flavours and shapes" — the variety that makes a display worth stopping at — while still producing in quantities that can be made ahead and kept ready.** **Job production offers something more attractive in principle: "customers the option of personalised chocolate lollipops."** **But the scheme's objection is fatal to it here: "lollipops are only small items so customers may not want to wait for them to be personalised and may prefer to buy off the shelf to make the transaction quicker."** **A product that sells on impulse cannot be made to order.** **However, batch production "could be more costly depending on the demand for different shapes and flavours"** — every change of shape is machine time that makes no lollipops. **I would still choose batch production, on the condition that the number of shapes is kept small enough that each batch is big — because a range of twenty shapes made in batches of ten costs more per lollipop than five shapes made in batches of forty.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **Batch production** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | **Lollipops sell on impulse** → variety to display, made ahead in batch |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Batch → **costly if demand for shapes is low**; job → **customers will not wait for a small item** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided the range of shapes is small enough to keep each batch big** |

**MS illustrations (Cadbury, MS PDF p.19; not QP wording):** batch production lets Cadbury limit or stop making Dinky Deckers if they are not popular and limited output may encourage customers to buy. Its cost is machine-changeover time. Flow production may ensure enough bars for customers and a continuous supply to shops/supermarkets; its risk is unsold stock if demand is weak.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose batch production for Dinky Deckers if Cadbury is uncertain how well this new product will sell.** Cadbury’s stated aim is to maintain customer loyalty by offering new products. Batch production would let the business limit or stop output if customers do not respond, and limited availability may encourage some customers to try the bar. Flow production could give shops a continuous supply and reduce the risk that interested customers cannot find Dinky Deckers, which could also support loyalty. However, batch production takes time to change machines, while flow production could leave Cadbury with a large amount of unsold chocolate if the product is unpopular. I would begin with batch production if demand is uncertain, accepting the changeover cost to limit exposure to unsold stock. If customer demand is already strong enough to justify a continuous supply, flow production may better support availability and repeat purchases. The QP provides no forecast or quantity data to settle that choice.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Maintain customer loyalty by offering new products, including Dinky Deckers. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Batch can limit/stop production and may encourage trial; flow can supply shops continuously and make enough bars available. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Batch changeovers take time; flow risks unsold stock if demand is weak. |
| **Possible evaluation** | Weigh demand uncertainty against the need for reliable availability; no sales forecast is supplied. |

**Alternative chain:** if Cadbury has evidence that Dinky Deckers will sell steadily, flow production may support loyalty by keeping the product available in shops. That depends on demand; the QP/MS does not guarantee popularity.

**On Lavender, the linked MS reasoning is different:** the choices involve local preferences across outlets versus a more uniform flow-produced range; do not transfer Cadbury’s launch-demand judgement to it. Irsi’s batch-versus-job choice likewise turns on variety versus personalisation for small chocolate lollipops.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** for Cadbury, production choice serves both the new-product loyalty objective and the risk of uncertain demand. Balance changeover time against continuous availability and possible unsold stock; do not treat stopping production as automatically more important than customer availability.

---

## 220. OPERATIONS — Kaizen versus just-in-time

**Two separate QP items share this production-method pair; the evidence below is item-specific.**
- **HRMW, May–June 2020 P2R, 2(f):** excluded from the 34-item review because the matched MS session identity remains unresolved; retain its source note only as an unresolved/out-of-scope item. Do not transfer its recommendation to Samsung.
- **Samsung, November 2025 P2, 3(e):** reviewed below against its own QP/MS.

### Samsung — November 2025 P2 Q3(e): JIT vs Kaizen to minimise costs

**QP wording:** *"Samsung has invested $17 million to build a factory in Texas, USA. It plans to introduce lean production in this new factory to minimise its costs. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: Just in time (JIT). Option 2: Kaizen. (e) Justify which one of these two options Samsung should choose to minimise its costs."* (printed p.15; identifier P78971A01520.)

**MS (p.19, 3/3/3):** JIT times electronic-part deliveries to laptop assembly and may reduce storage costs. Kaizen encourages continuous improvement, may reduce waste in laptops/TVs, and lower laptop costs may help Samsung compete with Apple. Risks: smaller supplier batches may increase delivery and total production costs; Kaizen takes employee time/training and may increase short-term costs. Level 3 requires detailed application, developed analysis and a clear, thoroughly evaluated judgement.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose Kaizen if Samsung’s new Texas factory can reduce enough production waste to outweigh the short-term cost of introducing it.** The MS says Kaizen may reduce waste when Samsung makes laptops and TVs, and that lower laptop costs could help it compete with Apple. JIT has a different cost-saving route: electronic parts arrive as required for laptop assembly, reducing storage costs. But smaller deliveries may increase material-delivery costs and total production cost, while Kaizen takes employee time and training in the short term. I would favour Kaizen if recurring waste is the larger cost and improvements persist; if inventory storage costs dominate and suppliers can deliver reliably without costly small batches, JIT may save more. The QP gives no waste, storage or delivery-cost data.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Minimise costs in the new Texas factory using lean production. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | JIT times electronic-part deliveries to laptop assembly and reduces storage costs; Kaizen may reduce waste in laptops/TVs and help keep laptop costs competitive. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Smaller batches can raise delivery/production costs; Kaizen requires time and training, increasing short-term cost. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare avoidable waste, inventory and delivery costs over time; the QP provides no figures. |

**Available ER/exemplar:** no item-specific scored candidate script or linked November 2025 P2 examiner-report commentary for this question is mapped in the local archive.

**Alternative chain:** JIT may be preferable if holding electronic components in the Texas factory is a larger cost than the cost of smaller/frequent deliveries and suppliers can meet assembly needs reliably.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** both methods address the stated cost-minimisation objective by different routes. Do not infer that a $17 million investment establishes a particular waste or stock problem.

---
## 221. RECRUITMENT — internal versus external

**Asked twice:** Kraft Heinz *(an internal appointee who understands sauce production vs external candidates via CV and interview)* — **Nov 2023 P2, 3(e)** · Premier Inn *(internal appointment within the worldwide hotels vs external via an employment agency)* — **Nov 2024 P2, 3(e)**

> **Both schemes carry the SAME two counter-arguments — learn them as a pair.** **Internal: "once that person is appointed it means there will be another vacancy … requiring additional recruitment."** **External: "the external manager appointed may not have the right knowledge and experience when they actually start work … which means they will have wasted a great deal of time"** and **"the new employee may not fit in with the other employees, which could cause problems within the work environment."**

**The AO2 anchors (Premier Inn):** an internal appointee **will have experience of how the hotel works**; using an agency **provides the opportunity to bring in additional skills of marketing the Premier Inn business**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(Premier Inn, November 2024 P2, 3(e); constructed)*
> **I would choose external recruitment for Premier Inn if the vacant position requires marketing skills the current hotel employees do not have.** The MS says an agency may bring additional skills in marketing the business and can select from a wider applicant pool. Internal recruitment also has a strong case: an employee from Premier Inn hotels around the world may already know how the hotel works, and the business knows that person’s skills. However, promoting that employee may leave their old job vacant and create extra recruitment expense; an external appointee may not fit with existing staff. I would decide from the vacant role’s actual duties: internal if hotel knowledge and proven ability are most important, external if new marketing skills are needed and the agency can find an appropriate fit. The QP does not specify the post, so neither route is automatically superior.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Select a suitable method for a new position in Premier Inn’s worldwide hotels. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Internal employee may know hotel operations and their own skills; agency can widen the field and potentially bring marketing skills. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Internal promotion may create another vacancy; external hire may not fit the team. |
| **Evaluation** | Match the vacancy’s duties to internal knowledge versus needed external capability; role details are not supplied. |

**Available report/exemplar:** no item-specific scored candidate script or linked examiner-report commentary for this November 2024 P2 item is mapped in the local archive.

**Alternative chain:** internal recruitment may be preferable if an employee already has the right skills to organise the hotel reception area; familiarity could reduce induction time, while the old job may still need backfilling.

> ⭐ **Premier Inn takeaway:** the MS gives examples, not a definitive job description. Do not claim the role must be marketing or that external recruitment always adds a capability.

### Kraft Heinz — November 2023 P2 Q3(e): promote internally vs advertise externally

**QP wording:** *"Kraft Heinz will need to recruit more managers to take responsibility for new production lines. It is considering two options: Option 1: promote existing employees. Option 2: advertise managerial positions externally. (e) Justify which one of these two options Kraft Heinz should choose."* (printed p.16; identifier P74635A01624.)

**MS (p.21, 3/3/3):** internal candidates may understand sauce production, reducing search time for a person with relevant skills/knowledge. External recruitment requires CV review/interviews for a Production Line Manager for Mac&Cheese and takes time. Risks: promotion creates another vacancy; an external manager may lack suitable knowledge/experience when starting. Level 3 needs detailed application, analysis and clear, thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would promote an existing employee if Kraft Heinz needs the new manager to understand sauce production quickly.** An internal candidate may already know how the business makes sauces, and Kraft Heinz may spend less time finding a person with the relevant skills and knowledge. Advertising externally could produce a wider choice for the new Mac&Cheese production line manager. However, reviewing CVs and interviewing takes time, and an external appointee may still lack the needed experience when starting. Internal promotion also creates another vacancy that Kraft Heinz may need to fill. I would choose internal promotion if the employee has management ability as well as production knowledge; if no suitable internal candidate can lead the new line, external recruitment may justify the extra time. The QP gives no information about the internal candidate pool.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Recruit managers to take responsibility for new production lines. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Internal candidate may understand sauce production and reduce search time; external recruitment uses CV/interview stages for the Mac&Cheese line manager. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Promotion creates another vacancy; external hire may lack knowledge/experience and recruitment takes time. |
| **Evaluation** | Decide whether a suitable internal manager exists; no candidate-pool or appointment-time evidence is given. |

**Available ER/exemplar:** local `2023-11_P2_Kraft_Heinz.md` contains examiner commentary and cohort performance for Q3(e), including the need to justify rather than describe; it does not provide a scored candidate script for this 9-mark item.

**Alternative chain:** external advertising may be preferable if Kraft Heinz cannot identify an internal employee with the skills to manage a new production line; it then accepts recruitment time and possible lack of product familiarity.

> ⭐ **Kraft Heinz takeaway:** internal promotion offers product knowledge but may displace someone; external hiring widens choice but does not guarantee fit. Use the new-line staffing objective, not a generic recruitment slogan.
---

## 222. COMMUNICATION — a written channel versus a face-to-face meeting

**Asked twice:** Café Pinch *(a daily-updated staffroom noticeboard vs a weekly staff meeting)* — **June 2024 P1R, 3(e)** · Rabanne *(email, sent anywhere vs weekly face-to-face meetings)* — **June 2025 P2R, 2(f)**

> **Both pairs contrast the same two things: information that is STORED against information that is DISCUSSED.**

**The AO2 anchors (Café Pinch):** a noticeboard **keeps chefs and waiters updated with the most up-to-date information about sales or meals achieved**; a weekly meeting **allows them to ask questions on any updates**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on Café Pinch)*
> **Café Pinch should hold the weekly staff meeting.** The decisive fact is **the split between the two groups the notice has to serve: chefs and waiters work different shifts and are rarely in the staffroom at the same time.** **A noticeboard is only read by whoever passes it — and the scheme's objection is exactly that: "unless all of the employees read the notices every day, or if Kumaran forgot to update the notices daily, then communication may not improve."** **A meeting guarantees the message reaches everybody who attends, and it does something a noticeboard cannot: "allows the chefs and waiters to ask questions."** **That matters most when the information is a change they have to act on** — a new target, a new dish, a new rota — because **a question asked now is confusion avoided later.** **However, the scheme's objection to meetings is practical and serious: "as all staff will be involved in the meetings they are likely to take place out of hours and some staff may not want to attend, therefore miss out on important information."** **I would still choose the meeting, on the condition that it is held at the changeover between shifts rather than out of hours — because a meeting nobody attends is a noticeboard with extra steps.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The weekly staff meeting** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | Chefs and waiters **work different shifts** → a notice is read by whoever passes; a meeting **reaches everyone and allows questions** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Noticeboard → **depends on staff reading it and on daily updating**; meeting → **out of hours, so attendance fails** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided it is held at the shift changeover** |

**On Rabanne the pair is email against meetings, and the scheme gives email the reach — "used by everyone and can be sent from one location to another" — with the counter that "if there is no internet signal then sending messages and receiving them would be difficult", and meetings the discussion — "people … give their opinion" — with the counter that "meetings are very time consuming and not everyone can make the meeting because of the distance between departments."** **Here the geographic spread is the deciding fact: departments that are far apart cannot meet weekly, so a relevant consideration may be the same shape as above but the answer flips — email, provided that anything requiring a decision is followed by a meeting for those who can attend.**

> ⭐ **Rabanne takeaway:** email’s reach and meetings’ discussion function both fit some communication tasks; test the actual connectivity and attendance constraints. This does not make written communication or meetings universally superior.


### Rabanne — May–June 2025 P2R Q2(f): messages/email vs weekly meetings

**QP wording:** *"Rabanne is finding that communicating with its employees around the world is becoming more difficult. Employees are also finding problems in communicating with each other. Rabanne is considering the following two options to help improve communication within the business: Option 1: communicate all information through text messaging/email. Option 2: hold weekly meetings. (f) Justify which one of these two options Rabanne should choose."* (printed p.10; identifier P78797A01020.)

**MS (p.12, 3/3/3):** email can reach employees across locations and keep them updated about packaging a new aftershave/production changes; meetings allow face-to-face views on Lady Million adverts and discussion of amendments. Risks: no internet signal can prevent messages; meetings take time and distance may prevent attendance. Level 3: detailed application, logical development, clear judgement/thorough evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose text messaging/email for Rabanne’s stated communication problem across employees around the world.** A written message can be sent between locations and may keep staff updated about production changes, such as packaging for a new aftershave. Weekly meetings could let employees discuss and give opinions on a new Lady Million advert, which is useful when a decision needs discussion. However, a lack of internet signal can interrupt messages, while meetings take time and staff may be too far apart to attend. Email is therefore preferable for routine information that all locations need to receive; meetings may be better for a smaller group when discussion is essential and attendance is practical. The QP gives no signal-coverage or travel-time data, so neither channel is guaranteed to reach everyone.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Improve communication among Rabanne employees in different locations. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Email can distribute production information across locations; meetings support face-to-face views and discussion about an advert. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Signal failure interrupts messages; meetings consume time and distance limits attendance. |
| **Evaluation** | Match channel to routine distribution versus discussion; consider connectivity and distance, for which the QP provides no data. |

**Available exemplar/report:** `2025-06_Second_Pass_Addendum.md` contains a verified 9/9 response for this exact Rabanne item. It is a scored candidate example, not a required structure. The document separately reports on the other Rabanne Justify question, but does not state its score.

**Alternative chain:** weekly meetings may be preferable for a decision that needs employees to explain or debate different views, if the locations can attend without excessive travel/time.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** this is internal communication across a global workforce. Preserve email’s distribution advantage and meetings’ discussion advantage, while testing both connectivity and attendance risks.

---

## 223. PLACE — physical stores versus online sales

**QP wording:** *"Apple wants to increase its market share in Mexico, Turkey and Saudi Arabia and is considering its methods of distribution. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: open more retail stores in these countries. Option 2: improve its e-commerce in these countries. (e) Justify which one of these two options Apple should choose to increase its market share."* — **June 2026 P2R, 3(e)**.

**MS illustrations (not additional QP wording):** the MS says more customers may visit stores to view smartwatches and may receive advice about apps/technology, possibly improving loyalty. Improved e-commerce may be convenient for laptop buyers with busy lives. Its counterpoints are the cost of building/staffing stores, which may raise prices or reduce demand, and that some customers may prefer to hold or view an expensive smartphone/smartwatch before buying.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose opening more retail stores in Mexico, Turkey and Saudi Arabia if Apple’s target customers in those markets value seeing and getting advice about its products before buying.** The MS suggests customers can view smartwatches in stores and may receive advice about apps and technology, which could improve loyalty and support Apple’s market-share objective. Improving e-commerce offers a different benefit: convenience for customers with busy lives who would rather buy a laptop online than visit a store. However, opening and staffing more stores is costly; if those costs raise prices or reduce demand, the stores could work against gaining share. Online shopping also will not suit every customer: some may want to hold or view an expensive smartphone or smartwatch before purchase. I would choose stores where customer preference for inspection and advice is strong enough to justify the cost; if convenience is more important to Apple’s target customers in these three countries, improved e-commerce may gain share more effectively. The QP/MS gives no data about local preferences, current coverage, or relative costs, so the choice is conditional rather than certain.

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Choice / objective** | More stores across the three named markets if local customers value inspection and advice enough to justify the investment. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Viewing smartwatches and getting advice may support customer loyalty; convenient e-commerce may suit busy laptop buyers and make online purchases easier. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Stores incur construction and staffing costs that may raise prices or reduce demand; e-commerce may deter customers who prefer to inspect expensive devices first. |
| **Possible evaluation** | The relative choice depends on customer preferences, current retail coverage and the cost of establishing stores in Mexico, Turkey and Saudi Arabia; the QP/MS supplies no comparative data on these factors. |

**Alternative chain:** improved e-commerce may be the stronger route to market share if customers in the three countries prioritize convenience and can assess Apple products online; it avoids the specified cost of opening/staffing stores. This does not assume that Apple currently has no stores or that every customer prefers online shopping.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** compare inspection/advice with e-commerce convenience, weigh both against store costs, and make the judgement conditional on customer preferences in Mexico, Turkey and Saudi Arabia. The QP/MS provides no local preference or cost data.

---

## 224. PLACE — choosing between two new locations

**QP wording:** *"Starbucks is continually looking at different locations to open its new coffee shops. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: open coffee shops in supermarkets. Option 2: open coffee shops in hospitals. (e) Justify which one of these two options Starbucks should choose."* — **May–June 2025 P2, 3(e)** (printed p.14; identifier P78796A01420).

> **Note that both options ARE viable — so the question is which location's customers will actually stop.** **The scheme's counter-arguments for both are worded almost identically, which tells you what the decision turns on.**

**The AO2 anchors:** supermarkets offer **customers who want a coffee after finishing their shopping**; hospitals offer **friends and relatives who have travelled a distance to get there**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would choose a supermarket location if Starbucks expects shoppers to stop for coffee after completing their shopping.** The MS also suggests they may buy cake, creating a possible additional sale. A hospital can serve friends and relatives who have travelled a distance, and some may take their relative for a drink. But supermarket customers may prefer to go home, while hospital visitors may have limited time. I would favour supermarkets where shoppers have time and the coffee shop is accessible on their route; a hospital may be better where visitors are waiting or can take a break. The QP gives no information about customer numbers, available space or time spent at either location, so the MS’s possible visits are not guaranteed.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Choose a location for new Starbucks coffee shops. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Shoppers may buy coffee/cake after shopping; hospital visitors may want a drink after travelling and might take a relative. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Shoppers may go home; hospital visitors may have limited time. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare actual opportunity to stop, access and customer flow; no location data is provided. |

**Alternative chain:** hospital sites may be preferable where visitors have a long wait or have travelled far, but that does not apply to every visitor and the QP gives no dwell-time evidence.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** compare the two customer situations without presuming that either group has time or appetite for coffee; the QP supplies no location/customer-flow data.

---

## 225. PRODUCT — a new product, a widened range, or a trial

**Asked three times:** Violet Cakes *(write and sell a cookbook vs introduce a vegan range)* — **June 2026 P1, 2(f)** · TADS *(teach other musical instruments vs offer online lessons)* — **May–June 2020 P1, 3(e)** · Rabanne *(product trials in a limited location vs advertising a new perfume worldwide)* — **June 2025 P2R, 3(e)**

**The AO2 anchors (Violet Cakes):** customers **who bought the last book may be encouraged to buy the latest**; the business **may respond to changing food trends by providing cakes suitable for vegans**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(written on Violet Cakes)*
> **Claire should introduce the vegan range.** The decisive fact is **what a cookbook does to the core business: the scheme's objection is that "if customers start to make their own cookies and buns, they will buy less from Violet Cakes, which may result in a decrease in sales."** **A cookbook teaches the customer to do the thing Claire sells for a living** — and the more successful the book, the more of the café's own trade it replaces. **A vegan range does the opposite: it sells to people who currently walk past the café because it has nothing for them.** **That is new revenue rather than transferred revenue.** **It also answers the trend the scheme names — cakes "suitable for vegans" are now something a café is expected to have, not a specialism.** **However, "the cost of researching and producing a variety of vegan cakes may be high, leading to high prices or low demand for the new range."** **I would still choose the vegan range, on the condition that it starts with three or four products rather than a full counter — because vegan baking uses different ingredients with different costs, and Claire needs to know the price and the shelf life before she commits the display to it.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The vegan range** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | A cookbook **teaches customers to replace the café**; a vegan range **adds customers who currently have nothing to buy** |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Cookbook → **customers make their own → sales fall**; vegan range → **research and production costs, pricing risk** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided it starts with three or four products, not a full counter** |

**On TADS the pair is a wider range against a wider delivery, and the schemes give range the revenue — "teaching other musical instruments will bring in new music students and revenue" — against the cost that it "would need to find and pay additional teachers", while online lessons reach "more students" against "technical issues that might lead to lessons being interrupted and not delivered, giving TADS a bad reputation."** **For a drumming school, the online route's failure mode is reputation: a lesson that drops out mid-way is worse than no lesson, because the customer paid and watched it fail.**

**On Rabanne the pair is a local trial against worldwide advertising, and the scheme's objection to the trial is the one to learn: "because product trials are based in a limited location, the results may not be representative of all locations in all countries."** **A test that only samples one city cannot justify a global launch** — whereas the objection to advertising is distribution: **"if they cannot find the perfume in a shop, then they will not be able to purchase it."**

> ⭐ **Rabanne takeaway:** for this item, keep the MS’s men’s aftershave trial separate from advertising a women’s perfume. Decide based on product feedback/representativeness versus worldwide reach and store availability; no universal trial-or-advertising rule follows.


### Rabanne — May–June 2025 P2R Q3(e): product trials vs advertising

**QP wording:** *"Over the past few years a number of new aftershave and perfume businesses have opened and may compete with Rabanne. To keep up with its competitors Rabanne is reviewing its marketing strategies. It is considering the following two options: Option 1: increasing the number of product trials. Option 2: increasing advertising. (e) Justify which one of these two options Rabanne should choose."* (printed p.14; identifier P78797A01420.)

**MS (p.16, 3/3/3):** trials let men test a variety of aftershaves and state opinions; amendments can be made before full production. Advertising a new women’s perfume can reach a wider worldwide customer base and may encourage purchase. Risks: trial locations may not represent all markets; a perfume unavailable in shops cannot be bought. Level 3 requires detailed application, developed analysis and thorough choice-relevant evaluation.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **I would increase product trials if Rabanne’s main competitive need is to improve a product before wider launch.** The MS says men can test different aftershaves and give opinions, allowing Rabanne to amend a product before full production. Increasing advertising has a different role: promotion of a new women’s perfume could reach a wider worldwide customer base and encourage more women to buy it. Trials may be less representative if they are limited to one location, while advertising will not convert interest into sales if the perfume is unavailable in shops. I would choose trials when feedback can guide product changes and is drawn from customers relevant to the intended market; if the women’s perfume is ready and widely stocked, advertising may better reach new customers. The QP gives no distribution or trial-sample information.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective/context** | Keep up with new aftershave/perfume competitors; options apply to different products/market examples in the MS. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Trials collect opinions and permit pre-production amendments; advertising may reach women worldwide and encourage purchase. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Trial location may not represent all markets; advertising cannot convert demand if the perfume is not stocked. |
| **Evaluation** | Product uncertainty supports trials; ready product plus availability supports advertising. |

**Available exemplar/report:** no item-specific scored candidate response is mapped in the local linked archive for this question. `2025-06_Second_Pass_Addendum.md` includes the other Rabanne Justify (Q2(f)); it does not establish this item’s score.

**Alternative chain:** advertising may be preferable for the new women’s perfume if Rabanne has completed product development and can supply shops; broad reach without availability risks lost sales.

> ⭐ **Takeaway:** keep trial quality and advertising reach distinct, and note that the MS examples concern aftershave for men versus a new perfume for women. Do not assume one option automatically applies identically to both products.

---

## 226. MARKETING — promotion versus product improvement

**Asked three times:** Can Culleretes *(more advertising vs opening for breakfast)* — **June 2022 P1R, 2(f)** · Four Fitness *(create a USP vs add video content to the website)* — **June 2025 P1, 2(f)** · M&M *(increase promotion vs add new car features)* — **May 2023 P2R, 2(f)**

**The AO2 anchors (M&M):** increasing promotion **means more people will know about M&M and the vehicles it has on sale**; introducing new features such as automatic door locking **may interest customers**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` *(M&M, May 2023 P2R, 2(f); constructed)*
> **I would improve M&M’s vehicles if customers need a product reason to remain interested in them.** The MS gives automatic door locking as an example of a feature that may attract customers who prefer the latest technology. Increasing promotion could make more people aware of M&M’s cars, vans and tractors and may bring them to showrooms to test or buy. But awareness alone may not lead to a purchase unless people drive or are recommended the vehicles. Improvements also carry a risk: design costs may raise prices and make customers less willing to buy. I would choose improvements if the feature is valued enough to justify its design cost and price effect; if awareness is the main barrier and customers respond to showroom visits, promotion may be better. The QP gives no demand, price or feature-preference data to settle the choice.

| Move | Item-specific reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Objective** | Keep customers interested while M&M seeks to remain the world’s largest manufacturer of cars, vans and tractors. |
| **AO2 / AO3** | Promotion can raise awareness and showroom interest; features such as automatic locking may appeal to customers seeking current technology. |
| **AO4 comparison** | Promotion does not guarantee purchase without driving/recommendation; improvements may increase design costs and selling price. |
| **Evaluation** | Compare awareness as a barrier with the appeal/cost of the specific feature; no evidence is supplied. |

**Alternative chain:** promotion may be preferable where customers are unaware of M&M’s range; awareness could encourage a showroom visit and test drive, although the MS cautions that this does not guarantee purchase.

> ⭐ **Item-specific takeaway:** do not turn the MS’s “unless driven or recommended” risk into a universal claim about how every vehicle is bought. Compare product appeal and purchase cost with awareness and test-drive interest.

**On Can Culleretes the pair is advertising against extending the offer, and the scheme's objections are both cost-based: advertising "can be expensive, so although revenue may improve it will be a cost for the business"; opening for breakfast means "as it is only a small restaurant they may need to employ more staff as well, but this could lead to an increase in costs."** **The deciding fact there is the customer: "an increase in advertising can reach new customers and tourists visiting Barcelona", whereas breakfast "can attract existing and new customers."** **New tourists cannot be reached by breakfast — only by being found — so the answer is advertising, provided the channel is one a visitor would actually see.**

**On Four Fitness the pair is a USP against a website upgrade, and the scheme's objection to the USP is the one that decides it: "if customers do not want access to this service as part of their membership they may feel they are being overcharged and the gym could be at risk of losing customers to its competitors, therefore decreasing its market share."** **An unwanted USP is a price rise with a label on it.** **The website, by contrast, is "currently for information only" and adding to it is a low-risk change — with the caveat that "advertising costs will increase to widely draw in customers", because a feature nobody visits the site to find does nothing.**

> ⭐ **M&M takeaway:** choose from the item-specific balance between awareness, the value of the proposed vehicle feature, and their respective cost/purchase risks. Do not transfer the Can Culleretes or Four Fitness decision to M&M.

---

## 227. STRUCTURE — a partnership versus a private limited company

**Asked:** *"Gianni Polvani operates Four Fitness as a sole trader. Option 1: a partnership with three like-minded health professionals who already work for Four Fitness · Option 2: a private limited company with shareholders."* — **June 2025 P1, 3(e)**

> **This is the only structure question in the tier, and the reason it is interesting is that the case hands you a fact that decides it: the proposed partners are HIS OWN EMPLOYEES.**

**The AO2 anchors:** as a partnership he **will have like-minded health professionals to share the decision making who already work for Four Fitness**; as a private limited company **he will have limited liability, as will his new shareholders**.

> **Model answer — 9 marks** `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
> **Gianni should form the private limited company.** The decisive fact is **who he would be taking as partners: the scheme describes them as "three like-minded health professionals who already work for Four Fitness" — which means the people sharing his decisions would be the people he manages.** **A partner cannot be given instructions, and a partnership with three employees turns a business with one decision-maker into one where the staff own the business** — and the scheme's own objection is precisely that **"the decision making may be wrong because the employees do not have sufficient business experience."** **A limited company keeps the roles separate: shareholders own it, Gianni runs it.** **It also gives him limited liability, "as will his new shareholders", so a failed gym no longer puts his personal assets at risk** — which matters more in a business with premises and equipment. **However, "he will have to find people interested in the business who want to become shareholders when setting up a private limited company, and this might be difficult as it is a small business."** **I would still choose the limited company, on the condition that Gianni keeps a controlling shareholding rather than an equal split — because a small business cannot afford a decision that has to be agreed by four people with three of them drawing a wage from it.**

| Move | The answer |
|---|---|
| **1. Choice** | **The private limited company** — first sentence |
| **2. AO2 + AO3** | The proposed partners **are his own employees** → authority and ownership would collide; limited liability protects his assets |
| **3. Counter-arguments** | Partnership → **the employees lack business experience and would share decisions**; Ltd → **hard to find shareholders for a small business** |
| **4. Possible evaluative consideration** | ⭐ **Provided Gianni keeps a controlling shareholding** |

**Second acceptable chain:** a limited company **can raise capital by selling shares, which a partnership cannot** → the gyms can be expanded without personal borrowing → **but a small business offering shares is asking people to buy into something with no market to sell out of, so the shareholders have to be found one at a time.**

> ⚠️ **The structural fact to carry into any ownership question: a partnership gives you partners, not employees.** **Where a case proposes partners who are currently staff, the argument against the partnership is not financial — it is that you can no longer manage the people who own half of your business.**

---

## ✅ THE 9-MARK TIER IS COMPLETE

| | |
|---|---|
| **Blocks in the tier** | **76** — derived from the 38 mark schemes, validated at 80 marks per paper |
| **Blocks carried by an entry** | **76 · 100%** |
| **Distinct exact option pairs in the inventory** | **76** |
| **Entries used** | **53** — numbered 175–227 |

**What the compression means.** The inventory lists **76 distinct exact option pairs across 76 blocks**, carried by 53 entries. The entries group recurring decision themes, but each mapped question still needs its own case and scheme. The theme groupings are navigation aids, not substitutes for the individual option pair.

**Every decision type in the qualification is closed:** sources of finance · promotion · pricing · market research and success measures · job design and motivation · contracts · training · production · operations · recruitment · communication · place · product · marketing · structure.

> ### ✅ THE COVERAGE IS VERIFIED BY SCRIPT, NOT BY HAND
> `python3 layer_c_9mark_coverage.py` reads the derived inventory, checks every one of the 76 blocks against a `COVERED` map, and prints **0 remaining**. Run it with **`--audit`** to print **each covered block's real option pair next to the entry claiming it** — the flag that exists because an entry headed *"venture capital vs a bank loan"* once listed a block whose actual pair was **venture capital vs crowdfunding**.

> ### ⭐ AND THE THREE THINGS THAT DECIDE ANY JUSTIFY ANSWER — the whole tier in three questions
> **1. WHO BENEFITS?** The business, its owners, its employees, its customers, or a country. **Several questions in this tier name a party that is not the business, and a contextualised answer about the wrong party scores zero.**
> **2. WHAT IS THE FAILURE MODE?** Every mark-scheme counter-argument in this tier is one: *interest you must pay anyway · a campaign that raises nothing · a facility repayable on demand · a reward most people cannot win · training the customer to replace you · a feature that raises the price and adds nothing.*
> **3. WHAT EVALUATION FITS THIS DECISION?** A condition, comparison, trade-off or other supported inference may help where relevant; none is compulsory. Keep case facts distinct from possibilities.

# BATCH 8 — THE 12-MARK TIER (entries 228–252)

> **38 blocks — one `4(c)` on every paper in the checked archive.** No verified P2/P2R 12/12 script was located in the examiner reports and exemplars reviewed for the 2019–2026 corpus; the five verified 12/12 scripts currently held are from P1/P1R. The P2/P2R models below remain `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` and unscored; this is not a claim about unreviewed or future publications.
>
> **The unit is the TOPIC, as in every other tier** — 38 blocks collapse to **22 topics carried by 25 entries**, because *location* alone appears on four papers and *quality* on four. **One essay per topic, with every paper's own markscheme anchors printed**, is what makes the tier complete; writing 38 separate essays would repeat the same chains a dozen times.

## 228. ⭐⭐ THE 12-MARK EVALUATE — use the exact question and level descriptors

**Asked:** all 38 papers in the checked 2019–2026 corpus have a Paper 4(c) Evaluate item. Each matched mark scheme reviewed for this corpus allocates AO1 = 3, AO2 = 3, AO3 = 3 and AO4 = 3; always verify the actual paper and scheme rather than treating that historical finding as a rule for future papers.

The command word appears in different forms—extent, importance, impact, benefits, how, why, whether. There is no single required paragraph architecture, opening definition, conclusion format, word count or sequence. Organise the answer clearly in whatever way lets you address the actual task and develop relevant, accurate reasoning.

### What the top level asks for

Across the checked corpus, Level 3 descriptors call for accurate knowledge, detailed contextual application, detailed connected reasoning, and a clear, justified judgement based on thorough evaluation. These are qualities of the response as a whole, not a checklist assigning one paragraph or three marks to each AO. Use the exact question's AO allocation and level descriptors.

The indicative content in a mark scheme is guidance, not a closed list. Relevant, accurate business knowledge and case-linked reasoning beyond its printed examples may be creditworthy. Keep the evidence types distinct: a case fact is what the QP states; a consequence inferred from it is reasoning, and should be presented as a possible outcome where it is not guaranteed.

### Evaluation without a prescribed formula

A supported judgement may weigh benefits against costs or risks, compare relevant factors, identify when an outcome is more or less likely, or explain why an alternative may be preferable. A condition can strengthen a judgement when it is relevant, specific and supported, but no particular phrase or named-condition formula is universally required. Nor does a conclusion have to sit in a separate paragraph or at the end if evaluation is made clear elsewhere in the response.

Pearson reports sometimes comment that bullet points or numbering did not help candidates reach higher levels on the particular paper discussed. Treat such comments as report-specific guidance, not as proof of an automatic mark ceiling across all questions or a substitute for the level descriptors. No independent fixed mark cap follows from format alone in this shared scaffold.

### A useful self-check—not a mark quota

- Have I addressed the exact wording and decision in the question?
- Is my knowledge accurate and relevant?
- Have I applied case facts and developed logical consequences, distinguishing stated facts from possible outcomes?
- Have I evaluated the relevant choice and supported my judgement with reasons from this case?

These are prompts for checking substance, not a required sentence, paragraph, word-count or conclusion pattern.

# ---- THE FAMILY THAT APPEARS MOST: QUALITY -------------------------------

## 229. QUALITY — will it give a competitive advantage, and can it be maintained?

**Asked:** *"Evaluate the extent to which quality will give **Violet Cakes** a competitive advantage."* — **June 2026 P1 4(c)**, cake business and cafe · *"Evaluate the importance for **NEXT** of maintaining quality throughout all of its manufacturing processes."* — **May 2021 P2 4(c)**, clothing and furniture, 70 countries · *"Evaluate the benefits for **TUI** of providing a quality service."* — **May 2024 P2 4(c)**, holidays, hotels and car hire.

> ⚠️ **June 2026 P1 carries two names for the same business** — the question paper says **Crumb** (owner: Alex), the mark scheme says **Violet Cakes** (owner: Claire). Same story, same paper code; the bank carries both. **Use the name printed on your own paper.** See `Source_Notes/ARCHIVE_LABEL_AUDIT_2026-10-14.md`.

> ## `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]` — June 2026 P1, Violet Cakes / Crumb
>
> Quality means a product is free from defects and meets or exceeds what the customer expects. At Violet Cakes it comes from high-quality materials — the fruit from England and Italy, the chocolate from France and Belgium — and from quality control, so that the cakes taste the same every time. Quality clearly matters to a business selling celebration cakes, but it is only one of the things that creates a competitive advantage.
>
> If the food served in the cafe is of a high quality, this should attract consumers to visit, and if the cookies and cakes are of a high quality this should lead to word-of-mouth recommendations. That is how a cake business grows without paying for advertising: a wedding guest who tastes the cake recommends the baker. It may also lead to more high-profile orders such as the wedding cakes it has supplied to celebrities, resulting in increased publicity and a larger market share. A reputation built this way is difficult for a rival to copy quickly, because it rests on years of consistent quality rather than on a price cut — and that is exactly what makes it a competitive advantage rather than a temporary gain.
>
> However, other factors such as the location of the cafe and the price of the cakes may be more important to potential customers. A customer choosing where to buy lunch weighs convenience and cost heavily, and may never taste the difference in quality before deciding. In addition, the cost of high-quality ingredients such as chocolate from France may increase the cost of the cakes, making them uncompetitive against businesses using cheaper ingredients, and if it raises prices to protect its margin, it risks losing the price-sensitive customers that quality was supposed to win.
>
> Overall, quality will give Violet Cakes a competitive advantage, but only to a limited extent. This depends on whether the customers who buy its cakes are willing to pay a premium for ingredients they can taste, and on whether the high-profile orders continue. In the short term the higher ingredient costs will reduce the profit margin, but in the long term a reputation for quality is the business's strongest and least imitable asset.

**The scheme's own content, per paper — use your paper's column:**

| | **Violet Cakes / Crumb** (2026 P1) | **NEXT** (2021 P2) | **TUI** (2024 P2) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **AO2 anchor** | high quality attracts consumers to the cafe; word-of-mouth recommendations | NEXT sells quality furniture and fashionable dress; a poor reputation means fewer customers in its worldwide shops | if holidays and experiences are good, customers return and book again |
| **AO3 chain** | reputation keeps it competitive against **Gail's Bakery**; a high-profile order (the scheme names the **Harry and Meghan** wedding cake) → publicity and market share | the same standard must be maintained by every manufacturer across **70 countries**; a poor reputation means closing shops, cutting outlets and sales | repeat bookings → recommendations to friends and family |
| **AO4 — "However"** | **other factors** — location and price — may matter more; French chocolate raises cost and makes it uncompetitive against **Flour Cafe** | maintaining the standard is **expensive** — constant inspection; and **once a poor reputation is gained it is very hard to regain loyalty** | good service to every customer is **expensive** — enough employees to meet their needs; once a poor reputation for car hire or lack of variety is gained, **loyalty is very hard to regain** |

⭐ **The three AO4s are three different arguments, and the paper picks one:** Crumb's is **price** (a more important factor), NEXT's is **cost of control** (the drawback of the same choice), TUI's is **cost plus irreversibility** (the drawback, sharpened). **A "quality is expensive" answer is not a substitute for the whole answer** — it is one paragraph of it.

⚠️ **The trap that turns an AO2 into a zero:** *"Quality is important to Crumb because it makes good cakes."* — no chain, and the business name is doing the work. **Build it:** *high-quality materials → a cake that tastes better than a rival's → customers recommend it at weddings → orders carry a premium price → a margin a cheaper rival cannot match.*

---

## 230. TQM — introducing it, and what it does to the factory while it is introduced

**Asked:** *"Evaluate the effects on **Saha** of introducing Total Quality Management (TQM) to its business."* — **November 2024 P1 4(c)**, robots for hotels, restaurants and homes.

> ## `[CONSTRUCTED GRADE 9 TARGET]`
>
> TQM should ensure that the robots are manufactured without any faults, and it involves training every employee in quality assurance so that waste and costs fall.
